We have thus shown the existence of a utility function. The fact
that this function is not unique does not detract much from it; in fact,
the difference between any two utility functions is only as to the zero
and unit of the scale — a difference quite similar to the difference
between the Celsius and Fahrenheit temperature scales. By
definition, the utility function, , is linear with lotteries (7.7). The fact
that, in a lottery with two outcomes, the first outcome may be given
any probability between 0 and 1 means that the range of the utility
function is a convex subset of the real line, i.e., an interval. There are
11 types of intervals; one of these, however, is the empty set, while
another, consisting only of a single point, clearly applies only to the
trivial case mentioned above (of an individual who is indifferent to
any choice). This still leaves, however, nine types of intervals, and
the question of which of these nine is the true utility space for a given
individual remains open.
7.3. Commodity Bundles
From an abstract point of view, utilities are assigned to all types of
events. This is as it should be, for players differentiate among all
types of events. On the other hand, from the economist’s point of
view, the natural application of utility theory is toward the possession
of commodities.
We assume a finite number, say , of basic commodities. These
commodities are held by different individuals in different quantities. It
is natural to use an -vector
 to denote the amounts of
these commodities held by an individual (or group of individuals). We
shall call such an -vector a commodity bundle. These commodity

--- Sayfa 219 ---
bundles are then treated as events: i.e., the event of holding a given
bundle. Thus a utility function will be defined on the commodity
bundles.
A caveat is in order here: we must distinguish between the lottery
which gives the bundle  with probability  and the bundle
 with
probability
, and the bundle  defined by
which is a fixed bundle and not a lottery. For instance,
 could
consist of two automobiles without tires, and
 could consist of 10
tires. Letting
, it is clear that the bundle  (one car with five tires)
is much preferable, at least for most people, to the lottery
One obvious condition that the utility function must satisfy is
monotonicity: if
, we must have
. It is also sometimes
assumed that the function  is continuous, and has continuous first-
and second-order partial derivatives (since it is monotone, at least
continuity and the first-order partials will exist almost everywhere).
The partial derivatives
 are called shadow prices (they
represent, naturally, the price in utiles that would be “fair” for the
particular commodity at the given level).
The jth commodity is said to be subject to the law of diminishing
returns if, for all
Most commodities are assumed to be subject to this law.
The ith commodity is said to be grossly substitutable for the jth
commodity if

--- Sayfa 220 ---
i.e., if a decrease in the amount of the jth commodity makes the
shadow price for the ith commodity greater. Since we usually have
, it follows that gross substitutability is a symmetric relation.
The ith and jth commodities are said to be complementary if
i.e., if an increase in the amount of one of them makes the value of
the other greater.
A commodity, say the nth, is said to be separable if its utility does
not depend on the other commodities, i.e., if there exist two
functions,
 and
 such that for all
A separable commodity is a natural one to use as a medium of
exchange. In some cases the function  is linear; if this holds for two
different persons, we say that the nth commodity is linearly
transferable between the two players. The importance of such a
case is that, if the units of utility for the two persons are suitably
chosen, a transfer of this commodity from one person to the other
will leave the total utility of the two constants. Thus the other
commodities can be divided so as to maximize this total utility; a
transfer of the nth commodity (side payment) is then used to correct
any inequity that this first process may have caused.
7.4. Absolute Utility
In the field of welfare economics, it is sometimes desired to know
whether a certain action “will help individual  more than it hurts
individual .” Now this cannot be determined simply by measuring
the increase and decrease of utility, which this action causes to the
two individuals, for we have already pointed out that the units of
utility scales are arbitrary and thus cannot be used for interpersonal
comparisons. The difficulty is that there is no absolute scale on
which to measure utilities for this purpose.

--- Sayfa 221 ---
Suppose, however, that the utility spaces of different people are
bounded intervals. Then there will be an absolute unit of utility: the
length of these intervals. We can normalize all the intervals so that
they have 0 and 1 for their upper and lower end-points. It is then
natural to compare utilities on these scales.
The problem, then, reduces to the question of whether the utility
space of an individual is bounded or not. This is, of course, an open
question. Isbell, who favors the bounded interval, suggests the
following arguments in its favor:
1. Assume that my utility space is unbounded above. Let  be the
event “nothing changes,” and let  be the event “I am boiled in oil.”
As the space is not bounded above, there must be some event
such that
But this means that
However, I can conceive of no event , which would make me prefer
the lottery
 to my present state in life. Hence, my utility space
is bounded above.
2. Assume that my utility space is not bounded below. Let  be
the event “nothing changes” and let  be the event “I win a million
dollars.” Now, if the space is not bounded below, there will be some
event  such that
But I can conceive of no such event
. Hence my utility space is
bounded below.
3. Suppose, moreover, that the utility space is not bounded
above. Then there exists a sequence of events,
 such that

--- Sayfa 222 ---
Then the lottery
will have infinite utility (the Petersburg paradox). Therefore, to avoid
this paradox, the utility space must be bounded above. Similarly, it
may be argued that the utility space is bounded below.
These are Isbell’s arguments for bounded utility; it may be argued
that the fact a person cannot conceive of an event C just described
simply means his imagination is defective, and that the Petersburg
paradox is precisely that — a paradox. The question remains open,
naturally. It can only be said that, while things would be simpler with
bounded utility scales, we can nevertheless obtain most of the
desired results without any recourse to them.

Chapter 8
Two-Person General-Sum Games
8.1. Bimatrix Games (Noncooperative)
We have so far studied, in general, only zero-sum games. In parlor
games, or in situations in which the stakes are small monetary
amounts (so that utility will be nearly linear with money), such
models are accurate enough. On the other hand, when stakes are
more complicated, as often happens in economic situations, it is not
generally true that the interests of the two players are exactly
opposed; very often, both can gain through cooperation. Such
situations are called general-sum games; they include zero-sum
games as a special case.
In general, a finite two-person general-sum game can be
expressed as a pair of
 matrices,
 and
, or
equivalently, as an
 matrix
 each of whose entries is an
ordered pair
. The entries
 and
 are the payoffs (in utilities)
to the players I and II, respectively, assuming they choose,
respectively, their ith and jth pure strategies. A game in this form is
called a bimatrix game.
It is clear that, in many cases, cooperation between the players
can work out to both players’ advantage. On the other hand, it is
possible that, even where this cooperation is mutually beneficial, it is
forbidden by the rules of the game (by, e.g., an antitrust law). We
distinguish, therefore, two cases for bimatrix games:
1. The noncooperative case, in which any type of collusion, such
as correlated strategies and side payments, is forbidden;
2. The cooperative case in which all such cooperation is
permitted.

--- Sayfa 226 ---
We shall treat, first, the noncooperative case. As in the case of
zero-sum games, we can define mixed strategies for I and II as
-
and
-vectors, respectively, with nonnegative components whose
sum is 1. We define equilibrium pairs in the obvious manner:
8.1.1 Definition. A pair of mixed strategies

strategies,  and

The problem of finding equilibrium pairs to bimatrix games has
been approached from different sides, and some success has been
obtained. The reader is referred to the bibliography for details and
methods. Rather than give these methods, we prefer to discuss the
difficulties with the notion of equilibrium pairs — difficulties which are
most apparent when compared with the parallel notion of optimal
strategies for zero-sum (matrix) games.

equilibrium with it), and so we consider optimal strategies rather than
equilibrium pairs. We give now an example to show that this is not so
for general-sum games.

8.1.4 Example. (The Prisoner’s Dilemma) Consider the game

first row, while the second column dominates the first column (if we
adapt the definition of domination, given above for zero-sum games,
in the obvious manner). Hence the only equilibrium pair is given by

--- Sayfa 229 ---
the second pure strategy of each player. This gives the payoff vector
(1,1). But, if both players play “wrong,” i.e., choose the first pure
strategy, the result (5,5) is much better for both. The trouble is that
each can gain by double-crossing the other.
8.2. Perfect Equilibrium Points
We saw above the difficulties that can arise with Nash equilibria.
Because of this, some substantial work has been done toward
refining the concept, with a view to eliminating many of the less
attractive equilibria, and thus avoiding (some of) the pathologies.
The first (and perhaps the most important) such refinement, the
perfect equilibrium, is due to Selten [8.9].
8.2.1 Definition. Let
 be an
 bimatrix game. Let  and  be
strictly positive vectors, with  and  components respectively, such
that
Then the perturbed game
 is a constrained game, with payoff
matrices
 and
, where the two players are constrained to use
mixed strategies ,  which satisfy
and
respectively.
The basic idea behind the perturbed game is, simply, that since
players are not perfect, each player is liable to make mistakes,
though perhaps with very small probabilities. Thus each pure
strategy will be chosen with positive probability; the perturbation
vectors  and  give lower bounds for these probabilities.

8.2.5 Example. Consider the bimatrix game
It is easy to see that this game has the two pure-strategy

first of these seems rather unsatisfactory as both players use

hand, (row 1, column 1) is nonperfect.
For this last example, the perfectness concept works rather well:
the equilibrium pair at (row 1, column 1) seems “unsatisfactory,”
giving a payoff of (0,0) and thus it seems reasonable that it should
be eliminated in favor of the other equilibrium with its payoff of (1,1).
On the other hand, consider the following example:
8.2.6 Example. In the bimatrix game
there are equilibria at (row 1, column 1) and at (row 2, column 2).
Here, the same reasoning applies as in Example 8.2.5, and we
conclude that the equilibrium at (row 2, column 2) is perfect, while
that at (row 1, column 1) is nonperfect.
In this case, the “good” equilibrium payoff (10,10) seems to be
discarded in favor of a much “poorer” (1,1). In fact, this example is
somewhat analogous to the Prisoner’s Dilemma, and is due mainly

--- Sayfa 232 ---
to the fact that the first row and column of the matrix are dominated
(albeit weakly) by the second row and column. Indeed, it is easy to
see that any equilibrium in which either player uses a dominated row
or column with positive probability will be nonperfect.
More generally, a mixed strategy
 of player I is said to be
dominated if there is another strategy,  which is at least as good as
 against all columns of matrix , and better than  against at least
one column. There is a similar definition for dominated mixed
strategy of player II. Then we have the following theorem:
8.2.7 Theorem. Let
 be an equilibrium pair of the bimatrix game
. Then
 is perfect if and only if it is undominated (i.e., neither
 nor  is dominated).
We will not prove this theorem. We will, however, make a pair of
comments. The first is that an analogous theorem for games with
three or more players turns out to be false. The second is that while
it is relatively easy to check whether a pure strategy is dominated by
another pure strategy, it is considerably more difficult to check
whether a mixed strategy is dominated. One technique for this
solves an auxiliary matrix (i.e., zero-sum) game. We refer the reader
to van Damme [8.11] for a general discussion of this problem,
including a proof of the theorem.
Instead of requiring merely that some sequence of perturbed
equilibria converge to the given equilibrium, we might require a
stronger condition:
8.2.8 Definition. An equilibrium pair

strictly perfect if for every sequence of strictly positive perturbation
vectors

Clearly, any strictly perfect equilibrium is a perfect equilibrium.
Thus the concept is definitely stronger. Unfortunately, it may be too
strong, because strictly perfect equilibria need not exist. As an
example we note that the game

--- Sayfa 233 ---

the two other columns; against this column, either row is optimal.
Hence the equilibria are all those pairs (and only those pairs) in

Subgame-Perfect Equilibria
A special type of equilibrium, the subgame-perfect equilibrium, refers
specifically to games in extensive form. For an explanation of this,
consider the game depicted in Figure 8.1.
Figure 8.1.

--- Sayfa 234 ---
It may be seen that if the game ever reaches position

1. This would give II a payoff of 1, so II, at y, should choose  rather
than . This in turn would give I a payoff of 3, so I is definitely better
off, at
, choosing
. Thus, by the process of composition of
subgame strategies, discussed in Theorem 1.4.5, the strategies
for I and  for II are in equilibrium. This is, in effect, the equilibrium
guaranteed by Theorem 1.4.5.
Consider, however, the pair of strategies

mentioned above,  is always better than

difficult to see that the strategies

The question is whether it is reasonable for these two strategies
to be in equilibrium. Specifically, in what sense can it be considered
“good” for player I to use alternative ? A logician might point out
that the formal implication, “If play reaches position , then it is good
for I to use
,” is logically true because the antecedent (“play
reaches position ”) is here false. On the other hand, there seems to
be something wrong with a strategy that tells player I to use . Thus,
while it is true that these two strategies form an equilibrium pair (as
may be checked directly), we will consider ourselves dissatisfied with
such an equilibrium pair. We express this dissatisfaction by
introducing a new concept, the subgame-perfect equilibrium.
For this purpose, we refer the reader to the definition of
subgame, as in Section 1.4, above. Then:
8.2.9 Definition. Let  be a game in extensive form. A pair
 of
(pure) strategies is a subgame-perfect equilibrium if, wherever the
game decomposes (at a node ), the corresponding restrictions of
and  are an equilibrium pair for the subgame

We note in this regard that since the game decomposes trivially
at its initial node, then any subgame-perfect equilibrium is an
equilibrium. We also note that, in the game of Figure 8.1,

not a subgame-perfect equilibrium since

pair for the subgame

--- Sayfa 235 ---
The obvious question is whether such equilibria always exist.
8.2.10 Theorem. Every finite game of perfect information has at
least one subgame-perfect equilibrium in pure strategies.
Proof. It is not difficult to see that the proof of Theorem 1.4.5 is
valid with the additional condition of subgame-perfectness. In fact,
the equilibrium guaranteed by that theorem will be subgame-perfect.
When information is imperfect, randomization is, as we know,
normally necessary. Unfortunately, it is not clear just how the idea of
subgame-perfectness should be generalized to mixed strategies. On
the other hand, generalization to behavioral strategies is quite
straightforward. Now, we know that even a zero-sum game need not
have equilibria (saddle-points) in behavioral strategies, let alone
subgame-perfect equilibria. Kuhn’s theorem (Problem 8 of chapter 5,
page 115) tells us that all games with perfect recall have equilibria in
behavioral strategies (in effect, with perfect recall, any probability
distribution on the outcomes attainable via mixed strategies is also
attainable via behavioral strategies). We then have the following
theorem:
8.2.11 Theorem. Any finite two-person game with perfect recall has
at least one subgame-perfect equilibrium pair of behavioral
strategies.
Proof. The proof is by induction on the number of subgames that a
game has. We omit the details, except to say that it proceeds along
the same lines as that of Theorems 1.4.5 and 8.2.10.

A natural question to ask now deals with the relation between
perfect equilibria (for games in normal form) and subgame-perfect
equilibria (for games in extensive form). Obviously, the use of a
common name for these two concepts suggests a close relation. In
fact, however, while the relation seems strong enough, it turns out
that there is no logical implication here. A subgame-perfect
equilibrium need not be perfect; a perfect equilibrium need not be
subgame-perfect.
To begin with, we note that any normal form
 game can be
represented by an undecomposable game: player 1 moves first,
choosing from among  alternatives; player 2, with no information as
to player 1’s move, then chooses among  alternatives. For such a
game, clearly, any equilibrium point is trivially subgame-perfect. But
of course many of these will fail to be perfect.

clearly not subgame-perfect in the extensive form. The trouble
seems to be that too much information (about the game) is lost in the
passage to the normal form, and so the imperfectness is somehow
lost in the process.
8.3. Evolutionary Stable Systems
An interesting application of game theory to biology has developed
over the last few years, namely that of an evolutionary stable
system.
Suppose the males of a certain species of birds can be divided
into two types: a bold, pugnacious type, and a mild, timid type. We
will call these, respectively, hawks and doves, though we repeat that
they are members of a single species, rather than two different
species. In fact, it is generally difficult if not impossible to distinguish
between the two types, except for their behavior when two of these
birds meet in a competitive situation.

--- Sayfa 240 ---
Suppose, then, that two such males meet in competition — in the
presence, say, of a prize, such as food, or a female, that they both
want. If one is a dove, while the other is a hawk, the hawk will
prepare for battle. The dove, as soon as he realizes what is
happening, will retreat, allowing the hawk to capture whatever prize
is at stake. We might assign a payoff of
 to the hawk, and 0 to the
dove in such case.
If two doves meet, both will ruffle their feathers, but will eventually
come to a (more or less) amicable division of goods. We might
assign a payoff of
 to each.
If two hawks meet, they will fight for the prize at stake until one of
them is near death. We can assign a payoff of
 to the winner (this
is not good as getting the prize without a fight, but better than having
to share it) and
 to the loser. Since both have (a priori) equal
probabilities of winning, the expected payoff is
 to each of the
two.
We may represent this in the form of a bimatrix game
:
It is easy to see that this game has two equilibrium points in pure
strategies, namely
 and
: The hawk gets everything
without a fight, and the dove gets nothing but avoids a dangerous
fight. There is also a third equilibrium point, in mixed strategies, in
which both sides use the strategy (.333, .667). This gives an
expected payoff of
 to each player.
Now, what meaning can we give to an equilibrium point in such a
setting? If these were humans in a dangerous setting (two
teenagers, say, playing chicken, or two national leaders engaging in
nuclear “brinkmanship”), it is easy to understand what the pure
strategy pair
 means: I has reason to believe that II is very
timid, and hence I will play very aggressively; II has reason to
believe that I is aggressive, and hence II will yield. In the case of
animals, however, we feel that the participants are not capable of
such reasoning. The pair
 would suggest that all competitive

--- Sayfa 241 ---
encounters are between a hawk and a dove; since the animals
cannot change their character to suit the adversary (especially since
the adversary types are indistinguishable prior to play), such an
interpretation is not viable.
Consider, however, the mixed strategy pair (.333, .667) for both
players. This mixed strategy can be obtained if the species as a
whole is divided into doves and hawks in a ratio of one dove for
every two hawks. In such a case, it would follow that, in a random
encounter between males of the species, each of the two
adversaries has 1/3 probability of being a dove and 2/3 of being a
hawk. The important point here is that the strategy is symmetric:
since the probabilities are the same for each of players I and II, they
can be assumed drawn from the same population.
Of course, a new question now arises. Granted that the (.333,
.667) strategy is feasible, why should it ever come into being, or be
preserved? We have just used animals’ irrationality as a reason to
discard the two (nonsymmetric) pure strategy equilibria; is this not
also reason to discard any type of equilibrium in such settings?
The exact biological answers to such a question is of course
much too complicated for a treatment here. Stripped to its essentials,
however, it resides in the Darwinian idea of evolution and survival of
the fittest, and says, in effect, that, in the long run, the gene pool of a
species will evolve so as to optimize something — usually, so as to
maximize the survival probabilities of that species in its environment.
(Note, moreover, that the species is part of its environment, in the
sense that each individual must live with the other members of the
species.) We claim that the equilibrium ratio (.333, .667) mentioned
above is optimal — a better word would be stable — in that sense.
In fact, let us assume that, over a period of many generations, a
ratio
 of doves and hawks has been reached. This is the
established gene pool. As mentioned above, the males of the
species will occasionally meet, and battle for a prize, with an
expected payoff

--- Sayfa 242 ---
To explain the stability of this arrangement, we suppose a mutant
gene tries to “invade” the species. We now distinguish two cases.

as likely as that of the other.
After a while, however, the mutant gene runs into trouble. This is
because we must think of it as competing against the established

A point to be made is that the definition of E.S.S. is a purely local
definition, which does not tell us what will happen outside a
neighborhood of the E.S.S. . It may be that, once we leave that
neighborhood, the mutant  turns out to be much more adaptable

8.3.3 Example. Consider the evolutionary system
It is easy to see that both pure strategies are evolutionary stable. For
example,

component. Thus in any environment close to , the second row is
always superior to the first row. Outside a neighborhood of

--- Sayfa 246 ---
however, the alternative pure strategy, (1,0), will be better. In fact,
the first row seems clearly a better strategy than the second.
Heuristically, we might feel that the second row represents an
evolutionary “wrong turn”: for this species, it would be better if all
members belonged to the first type. However, some time in the past
(when perhaps the external environment was different from the
present one), the species developed to give rise only to the second
type. Now any “first-type” member would come into an environment
consisting only of second-type animals and would have difficulty
competing against them. Thus the first type, if introduced at this time,
will simply die out. But if it were possible to introduce a large number
of first-type individuals now, they could thrive. Given this last
example, we might ask as to the existence of pure E.S.S.s. The
following is an easily verified sufficient condition:
8.3.4 Corollary. Let

We omit the proof of 8.4 as it is a direct corollary of 8.3. The
advantage of this condition is that it is easy to verify. Its drawback is
that, while sufficient, it is not necessary, and there is no easily
verifiable necessary condition.
In general, of course, we cannot expect pure E.S.S.s to exist —
bimatrix games do not in general have equilibrium points in pure
strategies. We turn instead to the question of existence of E.S.S.s in
mixed strategies.
The obvious question is whether such an E.S.S. will exist for all
evolutionary systems. This is actually two questions:
1. Will a symmetric bimatrix game (A, AT) have a symmetric
equilibrium pair (x; x)?
2. If so, will this x (or one such x, if there is more than one)
satisfy condition (8.3)?
We answer question 1 first, in the affirmative.

do much better by choosing the first row (all doves), with a payoff
of 8. Unfortunately, the second row dominates: even though
doves do well, a hawk would thrive in their midst and eventually
drive out the doves. It follows that the only E.S.S. is (0,1), with a
payoff of 3.
(d) For this system, the first row dominates the second. This is the
only equilibrium pair, and hence the only E.S.S.
(e) Here, the second row dominates the first row, and thus the
second row is the only E.S.S.
(f) In this case, there are two E.S.S.s: each pure strategy is a stable
equilibrium point. There is an additional equilibrium point

8.4. Correlated Equilibria
Let us consider the situation faced by two motorists as they
approach an intersection: assuming they reach the intersection
simultaneously, each one has the option of crossing without
stopping, or of yielding and allowing the other to pass. Clearly, there
is a loss of time (and utility) associated with yielding. On the other

--- Sayfa 252 ---
hand, insistence by both on crossing will lead to a large loss for both.
The game can perhaps be represented by the matrix
where, for both players, the first row or column represents crossing
without stopping, and the second, yielding. It is not difficult to see
that there are two equilibria in pure strategies, namely (row 1,
column 2), and (row 2, column 1): one yields while the other passes
without stopping. There is also an equilibrium in mixed strategies, in
which both players use (1/16,15/16), with an expected payoff of
for both players.
Now, under some circumstances (e.g., if one of the two roads is
clearly more important than the other, or if one of the vehicles is an
emergency vehicle), one or the other of the two pure strategy
equilibria seems very reasonable. In general, however, it is not clear
which of these two equilibria should be chosen and even if the
players (motorists) know which of the two is to be chosen, one of
them might feel unhappy about this. (“Why should I be the one to
yield?”) The mixed strategy equilibrium seems fair enough, but is
otherwise quite unsatisfactory. In the interest of fairness and
efficiency, then, and to avoid misunderstandings, a traffic light might
be installed.
In its simplest form, the traffic light is merely a randomization
device which, with a certain probability, tells each of the two drivers
whether to pass or stop. In a sense, this does not seem very
different from a pair of mixed strategies, except for one thing: the two
players’ probability distributions can be correlated. Thus, the traffic
light can, with probability .55, tell player 1 to cross (green light) while
player 2 yields (red light); with probability .4, it tells 1 to yield while 2
crosses; with probability 0.05, it tells both players to stop. This gives
an expected payoff of 2.7 to 1 and 1.95 to 2. Note that this would not
be possible with mixed (uncorrelated) strategies.
Of course, the two motorists are not bound to obey the traffic
light. (We assume there are no motorcycle policemen around to
enforce it.) Nevertheless, we claim that this pair of strategies

--- Sayfa 253 ---
(obeying the light, i.e., stopping on red and crossing on green, for
each player) is in equilibrium. We call this a correlated equilibrium.
To see that these two strategies are in equilibrium, we note that
since the traffic light never is green for both players, then player 2
knows, on seeing a green light, that player 1 is seeing red. Thus, if 1
is indeed stopping on red, it follows that 2 can never gain by
stopping on green.
Suppose, on the other hand, that 2 sees a red light. In this case
he cannot be certain that 1 sees a green light, but there is a rather
high (.917) probability that this is the case, and that (if 1 is crossing
on green) there will be an accident if 2 does not stop. It is easy to
calculate that 2 cannot gain by crossing on red. Thus, if 1 is obeying
the light, 2’s best response is also to obey the light.
In similar fashion, it can be seen that if 2 is obeying the light, then
it is best for 1 also to obey the light. We conclude that these two
strategies are in equilibrium.
It is important to notice, in passing, that if either one of the two
players sees a red light, this player is not certain whether both
players are seeing red. In other words, neither player has full
information as to what the traffic light is doing: each player knows
only the signal that the light has given him or her.
In general, let
 be an
 bimatrix game. A correlated mixed
strategy is an

This correlated strategy is to be thought of as a randomization
device which, with probability

given the signal to use , he or she cannot gain by disobeying, given
the conditional probability that this signal induces on 2’s action, and
similarly, 2 cannot gain by disobeying a signal to use .

Chapter 9
Two-Person Cooperative Games
9.1. The Bargaining Problem
We consider now the case of games in which cooperation between
the two players is allowed. This means that binding contracts can be
made, that correlated mixed strategies are allowed, and that utility
can be transferred from one player to the other (though not always
linearly).
There is, generally, a set of outcomes which can be obtained by
the two players by acting together. If we choose a pair of utility
functions for the two players, this set can be mapped into a subset of
two-dimensional Euclidean space
. Its image under this mapping
will be closed and bounded above (at least, the sum of the
coordinates will be bounded above). Moreover, since lotteries are
permitted (by means of correlated mixed strategies) and utility is
linear with lotteries, this image will be convex. The problem, then, is
to choose a point from this set which will satisfy both players.
Given a two-person general-sum game, then, there is a certain
subset  of
, called the feasible set. It is feasible in the sense that,
given any
, it is possible for the two players, acting together,
to obtain the utilities
 and , respectively. Generally speaking,
however, we find that the more one player gets, the less the other
player will be able to get (though this need not always be so). Now,
how much will one player be willing to give the other? How little will
he be willing to accept, as the price of his cooperation?
Though it is of course impossible to determine how a person will
act (in general, there are many personality differences to be taken
into account), we can nevertheless set a minimum to the amount that

--- Sayfa 262 ---
a player will accept for himself. This is the amount that he can obtain
by unilateral action, whatever the other player does. This is, of
course, the maximin value of the game for that player. Let us call
these two values
 and . Thus
(where  and  are taken over the sets of all mixed strategies) if the
game is a bimatrix game with matrices
.
We assume, then, that we are given a set

maximin values
. We want a rule which will assign, to such a
triple
, a “bargaining solution”:
We ask, naturally, how such a function  is to be defined. While it is
important to repeat that the outcome in any particular case will
depend on the personalities and bargaining abilities of the two
players, the following axioms seem reasonable conditions to lay on
any such function. (These axioms are those of John Nash.)
N1(Individual Rationality).
N2(Feasibility).
N3(Pareto-Optimality). If

Of these six axioms, N1, N2, and N3 are obvious and need no
justification. N4 states that, if a point
 is the solution to a
bargaining problem, and then the feasible set is enlarged, the
solution to the new problem will either be
 itself or one of the new
points in the set, not a point in the old, smaller set. As such (and,
also, in other contexts) it has been open to criticism. N5 is natural
enough if we assume that any utility function is as good as any other.
It is open to criticism if absolute utility (7.4) is introduced. Finally, N6
is quite acceptable if the bargaining is between two equal entities; it
may not be so acceptable if the bargaining is between unequal
entities, e.g., between one person and an entire community.
Several other axioms might be suggested for a bargaining
function; an example would be
N7(Monotonicity).If

Unfortunately, N7 is not compatible with N2 and N3. Because it is
difficult to quarrel with either of these, we conclude that N7 should be
discarded (though someone might claim N7 is not very different from
N4). In general, we need consider no further axioms because of the
following remarkable theorem:
9.1.2 Theorem. There is a unique function

In a sense, this result seems strange: it implies that the richer

an interpersonal comparison of utility, which is not generally allowed.
The fact is that player II’s utility for money decreases rapidly, while I’s
does not. The result is that II will be very eager to get at least
something, and can be “bargained down” by I.
9.2. Threats
A serious objection can be raised to Nash’s bargaining scheme, and
it is that it does not take threats into account. The following example
will best outline this objection.
9.2.1 Example. A worker has the choice of working, in which case
he will be paid a subsistence salary while his boss has a profit of
$10, or not working, in which case he will starve, while his boss
makes no profit. A reasonable assignment of utilities to these events
would be
 and
. The boss, however, may, if he desires,
give part of the profit to the worker, so that (assuming utility to be
linearly transferable) the set  will include all points below
,
and none other in the positive quadrant. As it is clear that
,
the Nash solution would seem to be
. This disregards,
however, the fact that the second player is in a much stronger
position than his opponent. In fact, player I cannot prevent II from
getting $10 except by taking a very difficult step; a threat to stop
work on his part would not be very believable, and as a result, he will
probably continue to work for his subsistence salary.
It is difficult to deny that an example such as this tends to weaken
Nash’s bargaining solution. Some analysis of threats is in order if we
are to correct these weaknesses. Quite generally, a threat is effective
if it is believable, and if it tends to improve the position of the
threatener vis-à-vis the person being threatened. Thus, a threat to
kill someone is generally more effective than a threat to become
angry, because the position of the killer is certainly improved vis-à-
vis his victim (though in point of fact it may be worsened in absolute

--- Sayfa 272 ---
terms) while getting angry generally does no such thing. On the
other hand, a threat to destroy the whole world, while it may possibly
improve the threatened position in regard to others (reducing them
all to equality in nothingness), is not very believable and hence not
effective.
Nash suggests the following three-step bargaining scheme:
1. Player I announces a “threat” strategy x.
2. Player II, in ignorance of x, announces a threat strategy y.
3. Players I and II bargain. If they can come to an agreement,
then that agreement take effect. If they do not come to an
agreement, then they must use their threat strategies x and y;
the payoffs to the two players will be determined in this
manner.
The question naturally arises as to the enforcement of these
threats; if one of the players makes a wild threat, he may find himself
averse to carrying out the threat later on. Some machinery may,
however, be devised for this purpose. At any rate, we assume a
player is bound to his threat in some manner or other.
In effect, what this means is that the maximum values
 and
have been replaced by the threat values
 and
. Axioms N1–
N5 are now applied, and the result is the solution
 where
 is
the point in  which maximizes
(subject, of course, to
).
A figure will probably explain the workings of this scheme best. In
Figure 9.5, a typical set  (closed, bounded, and convex) is shown.
The curve

exists, a line is drawn whose slope is the negative of the tangent
slope. If the tangent does not exist (as at point
) two lines are
drawn, corresponding to the right and left tangents to
 at . It is
easy to see that, by the convexity of

intersect, if at all, outside of .

--- Sayfa 273 ---
Figure 9.5..
Now, suppose the threat strategies  and  give, as threat value,
a point such as , lying on one of these lines. The arbitrated (Nash)
value of the game is the point  at which this line cuts
. If, on the
other hand, the threat value is at a point such as  (lying inside the
angle made by two lines from the same point in
) then the
arbitrated value will be the point  at which these two lines meet
.
Thus player I’s aim, in choosing his threat strategy, will be to get a
point lying on the lowest possible of this family of lines; player II’s
aim is to get a point on the highest possible of these lines.
To the question that arises as to the existence of equilibrium pairs
of threat strategies, the answer can be given that they do exist. (It is,
of course, necessary to define equilibrium pairs for such games; this
is done in the obvious manner.)

--- Sayfa 274 ---
9.2.2 Theorem. Any bimatrix game has at least one equilibrium pair
of threat strategies
.

strategies, however, are directly opposed. Hence it is not difficult to
prove the following theorem as well.

Theorem 2.1.2. By Theorem 9.2.3, we see that we can actually talk
about optimal threat strategies (rather than merely equilibrium

An interesting problem is that of computing optimal threat

might be a simple problem.
If, in particular, utility is linearly transferable between the two
players, the problem becomes quite simple. In fact, we can choose
the utility scales so that utility is transferable at the rate of 1:1. The
obvious adaptation of what we studied above tells us that, if  and

where  is the maximum utility that can be obtained by the two

the optimal threat strategies for the bimatrix game

9.3. Time-Restricted Bargaining
An alternative approach to the bargaining problem, due to Rubinstein
[1980], consists in treating the bargaining process as a game in
extensive form. We will assume a set of feasible alternatives,
,
closed, convex, and bounded above (as in 9.1). For simplicity, we
shall assume that the conflict payoff is
. We will also make an
assumption of nondegeneracy, so that
 contains some positive
points. We might then imagine a multistep process such as the
following:
(1) Player I “makes an offer” of v, i.e., suggests a point (u, v)∈S.
(2) Player II can either accept v, in which case the bargain (u, v) will
automatically come into effect, or counteroffer u′ by suggesting
the point (u′, v′)∈S. (Presumably, this would have v′>v.)
(3) Player I can either accept u′, or make a new offer, .
(4) Player II can accept v″ or reject and counteroffer
.
(5) –
 The offers and counteroffers continue. If the two can never
agree, the payoff will be (0, 0).
The bargaining can continue in this fashion indefinitely, until
finally one of the two accepts the last offer made by the other.
Presumably the offers would increase — or at least not decrease —
at each step, so that
 and
, but even
this is not, strictly speaking, necessary.
It is not difficult to see that this game has many equilibrium pairs.
In fact, let
 be any Pareto-optimal point. Let  be a strategy on
the part of player I to offer
 at some time, and never to offer any
more, or accept any less than
. Let  be a strategy on the part of II
to offer
 (or accept
) at some time, and never to offer any more
than
, or to accept any less than
. Then
 is an equilibrium
pair. The problem is not in the lack of an equilibrium pair, but rather
in the superabundance of them!
As in Section 8.3 above, such a multiplicity of equilibria suggests
that we must look for a special type of equilibrium. Given that the
game here is in extensive form, it seems reasonable to look for a

--- Sayfa 278 ---
subgame-perfect equilibrium. Now this is a game of perfect
information; unfortunately, however, the game is not finite (the
number of steps is unbounded, and there is possibly an infinite
number of alternatives at each step). Hence Theorem 1.3.1 does not
apply, and we cannot guarantee the existence of a perfect
equilibrium pair.
Now, one of the problems with the game as described above lies
in the fact that time does not matter: a bargain during the first stage
is worth as much as the same bargain many stages later. Thus there
is no reason for either player to yield in any way, since by refusing to
yield now he or she can still hope to obtain more in the future, and in
any case the same bargain will still be available later.
In real life, however, things are not so. In a strike, the outcome
frequently hinges on the relative size of the union’s strike fund and
the firm’s capital reserves, i.e., on which of the two parties can
outwait the other. In other cases, even when the situation is not so
critical as in a strike, still each bargainer realizes that a bargain today
is worth considerably more (has greater utility) than the same
bargain many time periods in the future. We consider therefore the
same model as before, but with this modification:
Each step (offer or counteroffer) requires one period of time.
(Acceptance can be immediate.) The two players have discount
factors
 and
, per period,
. In the primal game, we will
assume that Player I makes the first offer; in the dual game, player II
makes the first offer.
It should be clear that this modification changes the character of
the game considerably. In the extreme case, with

player I can offer a very small (but positive) . Player II would have to
choose between accepting  immediately, or waiting one period of
time — so that anything he can manage to obtain then must be
discounted by

advantage of the first move, has strengthened I’s hand substantially.
Consider, now, the primal game. At stage 1, player I chooses a
Pareto-optimal point
 and offers

between accepting , or refusing. If II refuses, then the game moves
to stage 2, with II to make the next offer. It is not difficult to see that
stage 2 of the primal game is strategically equivalent to stage 1 of

--- Sayfa 279 ---
the dual game—the alternatives are exactly the same, and the
payoffs (except for the fact that they have all been multiplied by the
corresponding discount factor) are the same.
Assuming, then, that II did not accept the offer at stage 1, it is
now II’s turn to make an offer, say

same (primal) game.
Since the several stages of the game are, essentially, repetitions
of either the primal or the dual game, it is possible to talk about
stationary strategies: in a stationary strategy, a player’s offer or
acceptance is independent of previous stages played. Thus, a
stationary strategy for I consists of a pair

Chapter 10
n-Person Games
10.1. Noncooperative Games
In considering -person games (where
), the same distinction
will arise as in considering two-person nonzero-sum games. That is,
cooperation among some or all of the players may be forbidden or
permitted by the rules of the game. Let us consider the first, i.e., the
noncooperative case.
In the noncooperative case, the principal question is the
existence of equilibrium n-tuples. This question is answered by the
following theorem:
10.1.1 Theorem. Any finite -person noncooperative game has at
least one equilibrium -tuple of mixed strategies.
We will not give a proof of 10.1.1 here. It may be seen, however,
that there is no difficulty in extending the proof of Theorem 8.1.2 to
this case.
Although Theorem 10.1.1 is certainly a valuable result, it may be
pointed out that all the difficulties which were observed for
equilibrium points of bimatrix games are also present here.
Moreover, the computation of equilibrium -tuples (where
) is
incomparably more difficult than the computation of equilibrium pairs.
In general, there is no great difference between the theory of
noncooperative
-person games and noncooperative two-person
general-sum games.
10.2. Cooperative Games

--- Sayfa 289 ---
Let us consider the case in which cooperation is permitted. We have
just seen that, in the noncooperative case, there is very little
difference between two-person and -person games. In fact, the -
person game is merely a generalization of the two-person case. In
cooperative games, however, we find that a new idea appears:
coalitions. In the two-person case, there is only one possible
coalition. In the -person case, there are many possible coalitions
and this means that, if a coalition is to form and remain for some
time, the different members of the coalition must reach some sort of
equilibrium or stability. It is this idea of stability that must be analyzed
in any meaningful theory.
We can see the meaning of this stability best by taking an
example. Consider the following three-person game: Players 1, 2,
and 3 are faced with the simple problem of forming a coalition. If any
two of them succeed in forming a coalition, then the third player must
pay each of them one unit. If no two-player coalition is formed, then
there is no payoff at all.
In this simple example, there is really very little to be said.
Assuming nothing about the personal relations among the three
players, any one of the three possible two-player coalitions might be
formed, and there is no way to distinguish among them. Hence the
three payoffs
,
, and
 corresponding to these
three coalitions seem to be a “natural” result, and may be considered
in some way a “solution” of the game. Alternatively, the payoff (0, 0,
0) which is the average of these three payoffs may also be thought
of as some sort of solution to the game. (We shall see later on in
what sense these are solutions.)
Let us suppose now that the game is changed slightly, so that, if
the coalition {2, 3} is formed, player 1 must pay 1.1 units to 2, and
0.9 unit to 3. This would seem to improve 2’s outlook, since he can
now gain more from the same action. However, closer analysis will
show that this is not so. In fact, the coalition {2, 3} now becomes
almost impossible to form (unless there is some external difficulty to
complicate communications between 1 and 3) since both 1 and 3
stand to gain from forming {1, 3}. Hence 2 is in a worse position than
before; in fact, one of the two possible coalitions he might join is very
unstable. He might, however, remedy this by giving 3 a side payment

--- Sayfa 290 ---
of 0.1 unit (assuming such a side payment is possible). This will
reduce the game to the previous game.
The foregoing example should serve to illustrate, first, the
importance of side payments in this game and, second, the need for
some sort of stability among the different payoffs in a solution. This
latter question will be treated later on; at first we shall deal with side
payments.
Once again, the existence of a linearly transferable commodity is
in question. Let us assume that this commodity does exist; the
theory developed on this assumption will be called the side-
payments theory.
In the side-payments theory, the utility functions for the several
players can be chosen so that the rate of transfer of utility among
any two of them is 1:1. As pointed out in the two-person case this
means that, if a subset  of the players (coalition) can obtain a total
utility , this utility can be divided among the members of  in any
way possible. Thus we will be interested in the total utility which can
be attained by any one of these coalitions.
10.2.1 Definition. For an -person game, we shall let
be the set of all players. Any nonempty subset of
 (including
itself and all the one-element subsets) is called a coalition.
10.2.2 Definition. By the characteristic function of an -person game
we mean a real-valued function  defined on the subsets of
, which
assigns to each
 the maximin value (to ) of the two-person
game played between
 and
, assuming that these two
coalitions form.
Thus

from the game, whatever the remaining players may do. From this
definition, it follows that
for obvious reasons. Now if  and  are disjoint coalitions, it is clear
that they can accomplish at least as much by joining forces as by
remaining separate. Hence we have the superadditivity property

--- Sayfa 291 ---
In dealing with two-person games, we have seen that we
generally dispense with the extensive form and deal only with the
normal form. This is because the normal form allows us to study
mixed strategies, which are the essence of such games, with
greatest ease. Now, the essence of
-person games is not the
randomization; rather, it is the formation of coalitions. Thus we shall
study, not the normal form, but the characteristic function. In fact the
characteristic function tells us the capacities of the different
coalitions, assuming optimal (maximin) randomization, and thus is
best suited to study coalitions. In general, we shall identify the game
with its characteristic function.
10.2.3 Definition. By an -person game in characteristic function
form is meant a real-valued function , defined on the subsets of
,
satisfying conditions (10.1) and (10.2).
Some authors have dispensed with (10.2) and studied functions
satisfying only the condition
. Very often such functions are
called improper games, whereas those which also satisfy (10.2) are
called proper games. Unless specifically stated, throughout this book
we shall deal only with proper games.
As mentioned above, the amount

game played between  and
. Let us suppose that the normal
form of the game is constant-sum (i.e., the sum of the utility payoffs
to all the players is always the same). The game between  and

minimax theorem holds. Thus we will have
This leads to the following definition.
10.2.4 Definition. A game (in characteristic function form) is said to
be constant-sum if, for all

--- Sayfa 292 ---
It should be noted that a game might be constant-sum in its
characteristic function form without being constant-sum in its normal
form. (It is even possible for the game to be constant-sum in its
normal form without being constant-sum in its characteristic function
form, though this cannot happen with finite games.)
Let us suppose, then, that an -person game is played. Without
inquiring into the particular coalition structure obtained, we might like
to know the possible payoff vectors. Now, assuming that some type
of understanding is arrived at, the players will have the total utility
 to divide. (In a constant-sum game, it is not necessary that an
understanding be reached for this to happen.) This can be divided in
any way at all, but it is of course clear that no player will accept less
than the minimum which he can attain for himself.

10.4. The Core
Let us proceed, then, to the analysis of games in terms of the

Theorem 10.4.2 shows that

characterized by a set of loose linear inequalities). This is most
interesting, since classical economic theory usually gives the core as
“solution” to most game-theoretic problems. Any imputation in the
core is stable, in that there is no coalition with both the desire and
the power to change the outcome of the game.
Generally, of course, the core may have more than one point.
This is not a great handicap; it simply means that more than one
outcome is stable. A greater difficult with the core, however, is that it
need not exist (i.e., it may be empty).

Chapter 11
Stable Sets
11.1. Introduction
We saw in Chapter 10 that the core—which represents a very strong
type of stability—is frequently empty. Different types of stability—not
generally as strong as that represented by the core—are therefore of
interest. The idea of stable sets was first introduced by von Neumann
and Morgenstern, who called them solutions.
In 10.2 we gave an example of a three-person game whose
characteristic function can be given most easily in the form:
We can leave the game in this form, or put it into
 normalization.
(This can be done by setting
.) The
normalization is a simple game in which two- and three-person
coalitions win and single-player coalitions lose.
For this game in
 normalization, we obtain the three
imputations:

--- Sayfa 319 ---
as a “solution.” Now, in which sense is this a solution? It is easy to
see that none of these imputations dominates any one of the others.
(It was this domination, in fact, which caused us to disallow the three
slightly altered imputations in the subsequent variant of the game.) Of
course, this is not all; any set consisting of a single imputation would
have this property. This set of imputations has also, however, the
following property: any imputation other than the
 is dominated by
one of these three imputations.
To see this, let
 be any imputation for this game. As the
game is in
 normalization, we know
 and
. Thus,
at most two of the components of  can be as large as . If this
actually happens, then both of those components must equal , while
the remaining component is zero. But this means  is one of the
. If,
then,  is any other imputation, then at most one of the components
can be as large as . At least two components, say  and , with
,
are then smaller than . But it is then clear that
 through the set
.
This gives us, then, a definition for our “solution concept”:
11.1.1 Definition. A set
 is said to be a stable set for  if

(i)
, then x  y
(ii)
, then there is some
 such that
Thus, a stable set satisfies the two conditions of internal stability
(no imputation in
 dominates another) and external stability (any
imputation outside  is dominated by some imputation in ).
Stable sets were first defined by von Neumann and Morgenstern;
they are often called solutions of the game. We do not use this name,
inasmuch as many other solution concepts have been formulated
since then.
One of the principal difficulties with stable sets is that, from their
definition, neither existence nor uniqueness is guaranteed. On the
one hand, most games possess immense collections of stable sets
(though there are a few with a unique stable set). On the other hand,
see Section 11.4 for an example of a game with no solution.

--- Sayfa 320 ---
11.1.2 Example. Let us consider once again the constant-sum three-
person game in
 normalization (and see Figure 11.1). As
mentioned above, the set
is stable. However, it is not the only stable set; if  is any number in
the interval
, it is easy to check that the set
in which the third player receives the constant , while players 1 and
2 split the remainder in all possible ways, is also stable. Internal
stability follows from the fact that two such imputations  and  differ
only in that, say,
, while
. But domination of imputations is
not possible through single-player coalitions.

--- Sayfa 321 ---
Figure 11.1.
To show that

Thus it is clear that, apart from the symmetric stable set, this

The preceding example shows that a game may possess a
bewildering multiplicity of stable sets. It is not clear how one stable
set is to be chosen from among all the sets. Nor is it clear, once a
stable set is chosen, how an imputation can be chosen from this
stable set. Von Neumann and Morgenstern suggest that stable sets
are standards of behavior, proper to a given social order. Once a
society has decided on the standard of behavior (stable set), the
individual
players’
bargaining
abilities
would
determine
the
imputation.
Unfortunately, as seen below (section 11.4) not all games have
stable set solutions. Below, we consider cases in which stable sets
exist, and special types of stable sets.
Let us consider the following theorems:

Theorem 11.1.3 states that every simple game has discriminatory
stable sets in which a minimal winning coalition excludes all the
others. In certain cases, it is possible that some of the discriminated
players will receive small amounts (see Example 11.1.2). The exact
values which they may receive are often quite difficult to determine;
we shall not attempt to deal with this question here.

11.1.4 Example. (Symmetric Solutions to Three-Person Games) In
Example 11.1.3 we gave all stable set solutions to the constant-sum
three-person game. We shall see how the symmetric, three-point
stable set generalizes to a symmetric stable set for other (general-
sum) symmetric three-person games.
For a three-person game in
 normalization, the values of the
one- and three-person coalitions are known in advance; hence, it is
only the values of the two-person coalitions that remain to be fixed.
For symmetric games, these all have the same value, and thus there
is a unique parameter,
, which determines these games. This
parameter can vary throughout the interval
 for
, we have the
constant-sum game (which we have already analyzed), while for
we have a “pure bargaining game” in which every imputation belongs
to the core, so that the core is the unique stable set.
When  is large (i.e., close to 1), the players may be expected to
behave very much as in the constant-sum game. Thus, they will first
attempt to enter into a two-player coalition. Since they are bidding
against each other, they cannot ask too much of their presumptive
partner, and the “natural” result may be an agreement, between the
members of whichever coalition forms, to divide their winnings
equally.
Once a two-player coalition has been formed, its members will
bargain with the remaining player to determine the division of the
remaining
 units of utility. In this case there are no alternatives;
there is no one else to bargain with, and so the amount which the
coalition receives depends only on the bargaining abilities of the three
players. Thus we may expect to find a stable set consisting of the
three line-segments.

fixed amount.

11.3. Edgeworth Market Games—An Example
We have concerned ourselves through this chapter with some strictly
mathematical ideas. We will now apply some of these to economic

--- Sayfa 339 ---
analysis.
Let us consider the following type of two-person nonzero sum
game: Player I has  units of one commodity while player II has
units of a second commodity. Neither has any units of the other
player’s commodity. We represent this by saying that player I has the
bundle
 while II has the bundle
. Now, the two are to trade
with each other, the idea being that both can gain some utility by
changing to the bundles
 and
, where
 and
.
Games such as this were first considered by Edgeworth. He gave
a “solution” quite analogous to the von Neumann–Morgenstern stable
sets. In fact, it is a set of distributions  such that no distribution in
is preferred by both players to any other distribution in
 but, if
 is any distribution not in
, there will be some
distribution
 in  which is preferred by both players.
Finally, for any distribution in , both players have at least as much
utility as for the “initial” distribution
. Assuming infinite
divisibility of the commodities traded, this set
 will usually form a
curve, and is called the contract curve.
The contract curve is shown graphically in Figure 11.5 by a
system of indifference curves. These are such that any two points on
one of the curves which are convex downward represent the same
utility for player I, while any two points on one of the curves which are
convex upward represent the same utility for player II. The contract
curve will then be the locus of points of tangency of the curves from
the two families, restricted to keep both players’ utilities at or above
the “point of no trading.”

--- Sayfa 340 ---
Figure 11.5.
In Edgeworth’s analysis he concludes that, if the number of
players increases, then under certain plausible conditions the
contract curve will “shrink” to a single limiting point. This is the
“market price.” We give below a mathematical interpretation.
Let us consider a market consisting of a set
 of traders.

members  of

function

12.1. The Shapley Value
The fact that no general existence theorem has been given for stable
sets of -person games has led mathematicians to look for other
solution concepts. One such concept is the Shapley value.
Shapley approaches his value axiomatically. We give two
definitions first.
12.1.1 Definition. A carrier for a game  is a coalition  such that, for
any ,
.
Intuitively, Definition 12.1.1 states that any player who does not
belong to a carrier is a dummy — i.e., can contribute nothing to any
coalition.
12.1.2 Definition. Let  be an -person game, and let
 be any
permutation of the set
. Then, by
 we mean the game  such that,
for any
Effectively, the game
 is nothing other than the game , with the
roles of the players interchanged by the permutation .
With these two definitions, it is possible to give an axiomatic
treatment. We point out merely that, as games are essentially real-
valued functions, it is possible to talk of the sum of two or more
games, or of a number times a game.

--- Sayfa 354 ---

an -vector,

These are Shapley’s axioms. It is a remarkable fact that these
axioms are sufficient to determine a value  uniquely, for all games.

Apart from the axiomatic treatment, the Shapley value, as defined
by (12.4), can be given the following heuristic explanation: Suppose
the players (the elements of
) agree to meet at a specified place
and time. Naturally, because of random fluctuations, all will arrive at
different times; it is assumed, however, that all orders of arrival
(permutations of the players) have the same probability: viz.,
.
Suppose that, if a player, , arrives, and finds the members of the
coalition
 (and no others) already there, he receives the amount
, i.e., the marginal amount which he contributes to the
coalition, as payoff. Then the Shapley value
 is the expected
payoff to player  under this randomization scheme.
In case the game  is simple, the formula for the Shapley value
becomes especially simple. In fact, the term
 will always
have value 0 or 1, taking the value 1 whenever

12.1.8 Example. Consider a corporation with four stockholders,
having respectively 10, 20, 30, and 40 shares of stock. It is assumed
that any decision can be settled by approval of stockholders holding a
simple majority of the shares. This can be treated as a simple four-
person game, in which the winning coalitions are: {2, 4}, {3, 4}, {1, 2,
3}, {1, 2, 4}, {1, 3, 4}, {2, 3, 4}, and {1, 2, 3, 4}. We would like to find
the Shapley value of this game.
To find

would be
. Note that the value is the same for players 2 and 3,
even though player 3 has many more shares. This is not surprising,
since player 3 has no greater opportunity than 2 to form a winning
coalition; the game (thought of as a characteristic function) treats
these two players symmetrically.
In a similar way, it is clear that player 4’s strength is greater than
his share of the stock, while player 1’s strength is less.
12.1.9 Example. Consider a game similar to that of Example 12.1.8,
except that the players have 10, 30, 30, and 40 shares respectively. It
may be seen that any two of players 2, 3, 4 form a winning coalition,
while player 1 is a dummy: he cannot contribute anything to any
coalition. Thus the value vector is
: player 1’s shares are

--- Sayfa 360 ---
useless, while player 4’s extra shares give him no advantage over
players 2 and 3.
12.2. Multilinear Extensions
One of the principal difficulties with the Shapley value is that its
computation generally requires the sum of a very large number of
terms. Thus, even when the characteristic function is easy to define
(e.g., a weighted majority game), evaluation may require a prohibitive
amount of work. Recourse is therefore frequently had to the
multilinear extension (MLE) of the game.
As we have studied it, an
-person game (in characteristic
function form) is merely a real-valued function  whose domain is
— the set of subsets of
. However, we can also interpret
 as the set of all vectors
 whose components are either
0 or 1. Thus
is the set of all corners of the unit cube in n-dimensional space. The
function , then, is a real-valued function defined on the corners of the
cube, and it seems reasonable to try to extend it throughout the cube
There are, of course, many ways of extending the function. We
shall extend it in a manner that is linear in each variable (though not
in all variables simultaneously). This is the multilinear extension of .

At this point, we find that we have replaced evaluation of the sum

is that the MLE approach has two advantages. The first is due to the
fact that, thanks to our probabilistic interpretation, limit theorems of
probability theory (in particular, the central limit theorem) can be
applied. The second deals with the behavior of multilinear extensions
under composition of games.

12.3. The Banzhaf–Coleman Index of Power
A second index of power has been suggested by Banzhaf [12.1] and

{1, 2} and {2, 3, 4). Thus

coincides with the Shapley value. This does not generally happen.

12.4. The Presidential Election “Game”
The present method of choosing a president for the United States of
America presents us with a very interesting game-theoretic problem.
As is well known, the voters within each state elect “great electors,” or
members of the Electoral College, who in turn vote for the president.
It is generally assumed (though in practice there are occasional
exceptions to this rule) that all of a given state’s electors will vote for
whichever candidate is preferred by a majority of the state’s voters;
thus a very thin majority in a large state (i.e., one with a large number
of electoral votes) will more than annul large majorities in several
small states. It is clear that such a “game” is not symmetric, i.e., not
all the minimal winning coalitions are of equal size, and certain
coalitions with less than half the voters can win. What is not obvious

--- Sayfa 397 ---
is whether the game is fair (in some sense of the word), or, if unfair,
which voters the game favors. We shall analyze the game, here, from
the point of view of both the Shapley value and the Banzhaf–
Coleman index of power.
We have here, in effect, a compound simple game
where  is the Presidential Election Game (i.e., the game among all
voters in the United States),  is the Electoral College Game (i.e., the
voting game among the great electors), and
 are the state
games, i.e.,  is the game played among the voters in the th state.
Let us consider, first, the Electoral College Game . Assuming (as
we have) that all electors from a given state vote together (the unit
rule), this is a 51-person weighted majority game with the symbol
where

as of 1977, from a maximum of 45 for California to a minimum of 3 for
the smallest states and the District of Columbia).

12.5. The Coalition Value
As we have seen, the values described above — both the Shapley
value and the Banzhaf–Coleman index of power — depend only on
the characteristic function of the game. This is quite proper if we wish
to know, in the abstract and with no further information, what an
individual player may reasonably expect to win in the game. Quite
frequently, however, there is additional information available. If
nothing else, the players’ identities are known; in many cases some
of the coalitions, while not certain, are very likely to form, while others
are seemingly out of the question due to the players’ previous
relations with each other.
In such cases, a realistic appraisal should take these prior
situations into account.
In the simplest of cases, we might imagine a partition of the set
of players into a priori unions

--- Sayfa 409 ---
where the coalitions (unions)
 are pairwise disjoint, and their union
is .
We can think of these unions as prior agreements among the
several players: perhaps they are firms. The members of each of
these unions (i.e., the owners, management, and employees of the
firms) have agreed to cooperate, and in fact they will do so in most
cases, though they are in no way forced to do so. Thus members of
each firm can always threaten to defect, and this threat must be taken
into account by the remaining members. We find, then, that a two-part
process takes place: on the one hand, the firms deal with each other
to determine how much each one receives; on the other, the
members of each firm bargain within the firm to determine how these
receipts are to be divided.
Given the partition , we can define a quotient game
 with
player set
, by
for
. Effectively, this is the game played by the firms; player  in
this game is really firm
 in the original game. In such a case,
 is
the amount that firm
 can earn in the game , acting as a coalition,
while
 is the amount that all the firms
,
, can earn (in ) by
acting together.
12.5.1 Example. Let  be the four-person game of Example 12.1.8,
where the players have 10, 20, 30, and 40 shares of stock,
respectively, with 51 shares (or more) necessary for a winning
coalition. Suppose players 1 and 2 agree to work together (perhaps
they marry each other). In this case  would be the partition {{1, 2},
{3}, {4}}. Thus  would be a 3-player game between the “unions” {1,

We can, perhaps, best understand these axioms by comparison

Axiom A1. C4 and C5 are restricted version of A2 and A3, saying in
effect that symmetry of treatment applies only within a given union
(e.g., two equivalent workers will be paid quite different salaries if one
works for a profitable firm while another works for a struggling small
business), and that the value is additive only if the same coalition
structure is kept throughout.

in effect, play the game

--- Sayfa 412 ---

the unions formed, but not on the labels given them.

Functions such as that of Example 12.5.11, which satisfy (10.1)
but not (10.2), are not, strictly speaking, games. Nevertheless, they

--- Sayfa 417 ---
will frequently appear in reductions such as this, much in the way that
imaginary numbers appear when solving algebraic equations. We will
call such functions improper games. It may be seen that most of our
solution concepts (in particular the value) do not require
superadditivity for their validity. Thus there is no problem in taking the
value of such improper games — as is, in fact, done when we
calculate the two-step value. The value of the reduced game
 in this
example is (1/12, 1/12), which is not even an imputation — but
Shapley’s axioms do not require the value to be an imputation. (Of
course, the value
 will be an imputation whenever  is a proper
game — but that is simply a consequence of the axioms.)
In general, then, even for improper games, the value can be
defined, and is in fact given by the formula (12.4).
With this in mind, we can now state the principal result of this
section:
12.5.13 Theorem. On the space of all pairs
, the two-step value
coincides with the coalitional value .
We omit the proof, as it is quite straightforward and consists
merely in substituting the reduced game into the formula (12.4).

Chapter 13
The Bargaining Set and Related
Concepts
13.1. The Bargaining Set
One of the difficulties with the concepts that we have previously
studied is that, in general, they do not seem to explain what happens
in any play of the game. Stable sets are given as “standards of
behavior”; the Shapley value gives some type of expectation. The
bargaining set is obtained by considering the discussion that may
actually take place during a play of the game. Thus we shall consider
the possible threats and counterthreats made by the several players.
13.1.1 Definition. By a coalition structure for an -person game we
mean a partition
of .
Heuristically, a coalition structure represents the “breaking up” of
the set
 into mutually disjoint coalitions. Suppose such a structure is
reached. We assume that each of the coalitions
 that forms will
receive the amount
 to be divided among its members. But how
will this amount be divided?
13.1.2 Definition. A payoff configuration is a pair
where
 is a coalition structure and  is an -vector satisfying

--- Sayfa 422 ---
for
.
We wish, then, to know which payoff configurations will be
reached. An obvious requirement will be that of individual rationality:
i.e., we may demand
A further possible requirement may be that no coalition
 will form if
one of its subcoalitions can obtain more than the payoff vector  gives
it. Thus
It is clear, of course, that (13.3) is stronger than (13.2). On the
other hand, the necessity for (13.3) is not as clear as that for (13.2).
Without going into the pros and cons of (13.3), we shall merely say
here that a payoff configuration satisfying (13.3) will be called
coalitionally rational (c.r.p.c.), whereas if it satisfies only (13.2) it will
be called individually rational (i.r.p.c.). We shall deal, first of all, with
c.r.p.c.’s.
Suppose, then, that some c.r.p.c. has been reached. When will
this be stable? It is clear that if the c.r.p.c. is an imputation in the core
of the game, it will be stable in the sense that no coalition has both
the power and the inclination to change it. Since, however, the core is
often empty, it follows that we cannot be as demanding as this.
Consider, e.g., the symmetric three-person game previously
treated. If the coalition
 forms, we say that the payoff vector
is quite likely. Nevertheless, either of the two players in the coalition
might demand more of the other, and threaten to join player 3 if his
demand is not met. Thus player 1 might threaten to form the coalition
, obtaining, say, the payoff
. Player 2, however, can counter
this threat by pointing out that, in such a case, he will try to form the
coalition
, offering 3 the amount , to obtain the vector
. Thus
player 2 has a counterthreat that “protects” his share of .

--- Sayfa 423 ---
To formulate this idea mathematically, we begin by defining the
idea of partners.
13.1.3 Definition. Let

Briefly, the members of

--- Sayfa 424 ---

is the set of all stable c.r.p.c.’s.
Now, the form of the bargaining set is open to some question.
Certain modifications might, indeed, be made. For example, we might
insist that only single players be allowed to make objections, or that it
should be sufficient for one member of

counterobjection.

13.1.8 Definition. The bargaining set

13.4. The Airport Game
We consider here an application of game-theoretic analysis to the
determination of airport landing fees. Essentially, our desire is to
develop a “fair” schedule of fees. We discuss here an airport, and
indeed the particular cost structure yields an especially elegant result;
nevertheless, such analysis should be applicable to the assessment
of charges for common facilities.
Generally speaking, expenses at an airport consist of two parts:
there is a variable expense, which is proportional to the number of
planes using the airport (though to be exact this expense will
generally be greater for the larger planes) and a fixed capital cost,
which must be amortized over a certain period. There is generally no
problem in allocation of the variable costs as these are directly

--- Sayfa 449 ---
incurred by the several planes; the problem lies in allocation of the
capital costs (e.g., runway and terminal construction) to the planes.
Generally, it turns out that the capital costs of the airport depend
essentially on the “largest” plane, which is to land there; this is due to
the runway. Once the runway has been built, no further capital
expense is necessary—until a new larger plane (i.e., one needing a
longer runway) is introduced.
We can now divide the planes into  types. The cost of a runway
adequate for planes of type

, where we assume

are  landings by planes of this type. Then

landings in set  is
while

Chapter 14
Nonatomic Games
14.1. Games with a Continuum of Players
In the attempt to apply -person game theory to economic analysis, it
becomes quite clear that small games (i.e., games with a small
number of players) are hardly adequate to represent free-market
situations. We want, for this purpose, games with such a large
number of players that any single player will have a negligible effect
on the payoffs to the other players. How large should this number
be? As large as the number of points on a line. (Generally, this line
will be the unit interval, [0,1].) This can best be defined as follows.
14.1.1 Definition. By a game for a continuum of players we shall
mean a -algebra,  of subsets of [0,1], together with a real-valued
function , defined on , satisfying

(i)
(ii)

As before, we point out that the elements of
 are called
coalitions, while the elements of [0,1] are called players.
One must tread more carefully here than in the case of finite
games. For example, the definition of (0,1) normalization must be
altered: it is not enough now to demand that
 for any point
, and
; this would be satisfied by letting  be equal,
say, to Lebesgue measure. But a measure is additive, and such a
game would therefore be inessential. Instead, we redefined (0,1)
normalization by these conditions

--- Sayfa 460 ---
A restatement of (14.3) is given by observing that, if
, we can

where the infimum is taken over all sequences of sets

that

measurable under . Thus  is a measure on . Now, it is easily seen
that  is the largest measure such that

In general, this measure, or signed measure,  will replace the
numbers

any (signed) measure  such that

The domination relation must also be redefined. In fact, to have

--- Sayfa 461 ---
