This effectively allows us to dispense with “small” (e.g., finite) sets.
With these definitions to imputation and domination, stable sets
can be defined exactly as for finite games. In general, not much is
known about stable sets for such games — just as little is known
about stable sets for, -person games, when  is large — except for
special types. In particular, stable sets will exist for simple games: if
 is a minimal winning coalition, the set of all imputations  such that
 on
 will be stable. (We assume (0,1) normalization here.)
Although little work has been done on stable sets, much work has
been done on the Shapley value of games for a continuum of
players.
Generally, the Axioms S1–S3 given in the definition of the
Shapley value in Chapter 12 can be adapted directly, mutatis
mutandis. A certain difficulty arises, however, with S2 (the symmetry
axiom); the permutations of
, which were treated there, must be
replaced by one–one measurable transformations of [0,1] into itself.
While this might not, at first sight, present any problems, we shall
see that it leads to a contradiction in certain degenerate cases.
The actual determination of the value for a particular game, of
course, presents considerable difficulty, inasmuch as the formula is
not adaptable to the infinite case.
Generally, the method used to determine the value for an
arbitrary game is to partition the interval [0,1] into subsets (not
necessarily subintervals). These subsets are then treated as single
players and the Shapley value for the corresponding finite game is
calculated. Finer and finer partitions of [0,1] are taken, and the
Shapley value
 (an additive set function rather than a vector) is
then defined as the limit of the values for these finite games (if this
limit exists).

--- Sayfa 462 ---
Let us suppose, now, that there is a measure  defined on  such
that
 depends merely on

. Then, by the symmetry axiom, if

have

follows that

, divided by a
constant factor to ensure that

14.3. Internal Telephone Billing Rates — An
Example
As a possible application of nonatomic game theory, let us consider
the problem of allocating the costs of telephone calls made from a
certain large institution. For cost accounting purposes, it is desirable
to charge the calls to the several projects or contracts that the
institution is undertaking; this may be especially important in case of
a research institute or university, where several projects may be
going on independently of each other, and “fairness” in billing is
desired.
Given the present policy of the telephone companies, long-
distance telephone calls can be purchased in any of several ways.
The “normal” method is direct distance dialing (DDD), according to
which each call is billed individually. Individual home owners and
small companies usually obtain their long-distance calls in this
manner as it is relatively inexpensive for small users. As the number
of calls increases, however, the institution will normally find it more
economical to obtain either a direct line to a distant city (e.g.,
Washington, where it wishes contact with funding agencies) or Wide

--- Sayfa 484 ---
Area Telephone Service (WATS), which, for a sizable initial payment,
allows a certain number of calls within a given zone; calls above this
basic number are billed individually.
In reality, there are two problems here. First, the institution must
determine the system configuration, which will minimize total cost;
second, it must allocate these costs internally to the several projects
using the telephone system. The first is not, strictly speaking, a
game-theoretic problem; its solution is, however, necessary if we are
to solve the game-theoretic problem of cost allocation.
Essentially, it may be found that, for a given choice of
configuration (e.g., two WATS lines plus one direct line to another
city), total costs will depend not only on the total number (and length)
of calls made to each destination, but also on the times at which
these calls are made. This is due to the fact that, if too many calls
are made simultaneously, the WATS lines will be unable to
accommodate them all; hence, some will be routed as DDD calls,
with a consequently higher cost. Typically, we can divide the day into
24 time periods and let
 denote the total minutes of calls in time
period  to destination . Then the cost to the institution, given the th
configuration, is given by
where the function
 is absolutely continuous and piecewise, at
least, continuously differentiable.
For optimal choice of the system configuration, costs will be
and once again  will be absolutely continuous in the
 variables
,
with — at least — one-sided derivatives everywhere.
We are now in a position to allocate the costs. Given the nature
of the institution, the following seem reasonable criteria for
allocation:
1. Total billings to the several accounts should be just enough to
cover the total costs of the service.

--- Sayfa 485 ---
2. All calls to a given destination in a given time period should be
billed at the same rate, regardless of the person making them.
3. All calls should be charged at least a non-negative amount.
It may, of course, be seen that these correspond to three of the
four axioms, which characterize the value for nonatomic games. We
may therefore represent this as a nonatomic game in which each
moment of telephone call is to be treated as a player. The value of
this nonatomic game can then be obtained by the use of formula
(14.25).
Thus, if we set

where

in the jth time period, chargeable to account .
Table 14.1 shows examples of these rates, to two different
destinations, over the day. As can be seen, these are relatively low
during the night hours, then increase during the day to reach a peak
when the frequency of calls is greatest.

Chapter 15
Games without Side Payments
15.1. Introduction
Throughout the last five chapters, we have made the implicit
assumption that utility is freely transferable among members of a
coalition. This is, in particular, possible in the presence of an “ideal
money”—i.e., a commodity whose utility is directly proportional to
quantity, and independent of any other assets, or liabilities, which a
player may have. Whatever the realism of such a situation, its
principal effect is to simplify enormously the description of the game
(in characteristic function form). In fact, a single number
 is fully
adequate to describe the coalition’s possibilities: it can obtain this
total amount in some manner, and then make side payments of
money to satisfy any complaints that its members might make.
In general, unfortunately, the situation is not this simple. Even if
money is available in large amounts (which is not always the case),
we find that the several players’ utility for money is not linear (e.g.,
the utility of the 1st dollar is much greater than that of the 1001st),
and not always independent of their other assets. In some cases,
side payments may even be forbidden (e.g., by antitrust or
antikickback laws). This being so, we must represent each coalition’s
possibilities, not by a single number, but rather by a set: the set of all
points that the coalition can obtain for its members (cf. our treatment
of the two-person bargaining game in Chapter 9).
An important concept here will be Pareto-optimality. This was
discussed, in the two-person context, in Chapter 9. More generally,
we will say that the point
 is Pareto-optimal over the set
 if there is no
 such that
, for all , with inequality holding

--- Sayfa 491 ---
for at least one value of . We will also say that  is Pareto-optimal
for the coalition  over the set , if there is no
 such that
for all
, with inequality holding for at least one
.
For any
, we shall let
 denote the set of all jointly
correlated mixed strategies for the coalition . Then, an (expected)
outcome
 will be determined by a choice of strategies
, for any partition
, of the
player set
. There are, now, two possible meanings to the
statement that “the coalition can obtain [some point] for its
members.” These correspond to the notions of -effectiveness and -
effectiveness.
15.1.1 Definition. The coalition S is -effective for the vector  if
there is a strategy
 such that, for any
15.1.2 Definition. The coalition
 is
-effective for

strategy
, there is some

It is easy to see that -effectiveness implies -effectiveness. The
converse, however, is not generally true. It certainly holds for finite
two-person games (this is the minimax theorem), but even for infinite
two-person games, it fails to hold. Assuming finiteness, it may be
seen that the two types of effectiveness are equivalent if utility can
be transferred linearly among members of .
Whichever definition of effectiveness is used, the set v(s) can
then be described as the set of all payoff vectors for which
 is
effective. We have then
15.1.3 Definition. An -person game without side payments is a pair

Some work has been done on stable sets: Stearns [15.15] has
proved that all three-person games have stable sets, and has
moreover catalogued all such sets. On the other hand, he has also
given a seven-person game with no stable sets (cf. the case of
games with side payments, where a 10-person game without stable
sets has been constructed).

15.2.5 Definition. For a given matrix , two primitive sets of columns
are adjacent if they have

Returning to Example 15.2.4, we notice that the set {1, 3, 6} is
adjacent to {1, 4, 6}. In turn, {1, 4, 6} is adjacent to {1, 4, 5}. On the
other hand, {1, 3, 6} and {1, 4, 5} are not adjacent.

--- Sayfa 502 ---
It is, of course, clear that a given matrix  might have no primitive
sets of columns. Even if a matrix has a primitive set, there may be no
primitive set adjacent to this. Nevertheless, adjacent primitive sets
frequently exist. Assuming that a primitive set exists, we can remove
one of the columns, and see whether any of the remaining columns
in the matrix can be introduced so as to form the adjacent set. The
situation is of course very similar to that found in linear programming,
where, at each pivot-step, one of the basic variables is removed from
the basis (i.e., the set of basic variables) and replaced by one of the
non-basic variables, to form an adjacent basis, i.e., give an adjacent
basic feasible point of the program. We shall, below, see the
importance of this similarity.
Once again returning to Example 15.2.4, let us consider the
primitive set {1, 4, 6}, giving rise to the matrix
Let us suppose we wish to remove column 6 (the third column of )
from this set. Which column should be introduced to replace it? Or,
to put it another way, we wish to form an adjacent primitive set,
, where
. What choice we have for ? The reader may
verify directly that the only possible replacement is
, giving the
new primitive set {1, 4, 5}. Similarly, if we chose to remove column 4,
the only possible replacement would be column 3, giving the
primitive set {1, 3, 6}. On the other hand, if, from {1, 4, 5}, we remove
column 4, we find there is no possible replacement: no adjacent
primitive set is to be found in the “direction.”
Of course, there are matrices for which more than one
replacement may exist. In the “very degenerate” case where all
columns are equal, we find that any set of  columns is primitive, so
there is a wide choice of replacement. This, however, happens only
because of equalities among the entries, as the following lemma
shows:

At this point, the reader should notice the similarity between the
present process of choosing adjacent primitive sets and the pivot-
step of linear programming. In the present case, one of the columns
in the set  is dropped; this will give rise to a unique replacement (if
any). In pivot steps, a nonbasic variable is chosen. This will
determine (see rules 3.5.2) a basic variable to be removed from the
basis. In the nondegenerate case, where no ties are permitted, this
basic variable is uniquely determined.
In both processes, of course, it may be that the step is
impossible. This happens, in the present process, in case no column
satisfies (15.9)–(15.11); in linear programming, it will happen if there
are no positive entries in the chosen column — which means that the
constraint set is unbounded.
If we know that the constraint set is bounded, then a pivot-step is
always possible. Similarly, for the present process, there is one case
in which we can guarantee that a replacement column always exists.

At alternating steps, one or the other of  and  is changed while
the other is kept fixed. Since there are only a finite number of
possible pairs, one of three things will eventually happen:

(α) some pair (y; J) is fully labeled;
(β) it is impossible to take a step;
(γ) some pair will be repeated.

first room of the sequence, however, the pair
, has only one
door. It is clear, then, that no repetition is possible. For, the first room
to be repeated would be entered through a third door — unless it be
, which would then be entered through a second door. This is
impossible, and so there can be no repetition.
An alternative way of explaining the above is to say that a
sequence of rooms, each of which has at most two doors, while the
first has only one door, must eventually lead to some other room with
only one door — or to the outside of the house.

15.3. Market Games
An important class of
-person games is that dealing with
oligopolistic market situations. In the simplest case, we assume that

The core is, of course, an equilibrium concept: points in the core
are in equilibrium, in the sense that no coalition has both the reason
and the capacity to disrupt them. Economists are interested,
however, in a somewhat stronger notion of equilibrium — one
created by a price system.
Let us suppose that a system of prices is given for our
commodities. This is not generally meant to imply the existence of
money; rather, the ratios of these prices represent rates at which the
commodities can be interchanged. Let

15.3.3 Definition. A competitive equilibrium is a pair
, where
 is a price vector, and
 is an allocation
satisfying
and such that, for each
subject to
We can see the importance of an equilibrium: a set of prices is
given. Each player then trades so as to maximize his utility, subject
to the obvious budget constraint that the total market value of his
final bundle be no greater than that of his initial endowment. If the
prices have been “properly” chosen, i.e., if they are equilibrium
prices, this process will just clear the market — there will be just
enough of each commodity to satisfy every trader’s demand, and no
more. If, on the other hand, the “wrong” set of prices is chosen, then
there will be some imbalance in the market: there will be an excess
demand for some commodities (which had been priced too low) and
an excess supply of some other commodities (which are priced too
high). The practical importance of this, for an economy based on a
free market, is easy to understand; the obvious question is whether
such an equilibrium exists.

--- Sayfa 522 ---

15.4. Approaches to the Value
As seen in the previous sections, certain satisfactory results have
been obtained in generalizing the notions of stable sets and core to
games without side payments. As regards the value, results are not
nearly so pleasant.

--- Sayfa 528 ---
Generally speaking, the situation is as follows. For the side-
payments case, the Shapley value is generally a well-established
concept. For two-person games, the Nash value is also well
established. As it happens, these two coincide for two-person games
with side payments (at least in the fixed threat case). What we would
like is something that generalizes both the Nash and Shapley values
for
-person games without side payments. There seem to be
several ways of doing this; none, unfortunately, seems wholly
satisfactory.
We saw in Chapter 9 that, if the utility transformation (9.3) is
made, the Nash solution to the resulting game is obtained simply by
an equal division of the possible increments in utility, and this would
be the case even if side payments were allowed. One possible
approach, in the
-person case, is to look for some linear
transformation of utilities, which will somehow allow us to solve the
game as though it were a game with side payments.
More precisely, let  be a game without side payments. We can
transform this into a game with side payments  by setting

15.5. The Bargaining Sets
We consider, finally, the problem of generalizing the several
concepts introduced in Chapter 13: bargaining sets, kernel,
nucleolus. It turns out that both the kernel and the nucleolus are
defined through an explicit interpersonal comparison of utilities,
which while not entirely intuitive, was at least permissible so long as
there was a common unit of utility. For games without side payments,
it is difficult to see how these ideas can be generalized.

free transferability of utility.

Chapter 16
Spatial Games
16.1. The Core and Near-Core Solution Concepts
In their original development of -person games, von Neumann and
Morgenstern represented all outcomes as payoff vectors, i.e., points
in -dimensional utility space. This representation in utility space
continued even after new models for -person games, e.g., games
without side payments, were developed. This is the representation
which we have used, up to this point, in this book.
An alternative representation of outcomes in terms of policy
space is possible, however. In this model, outcomes of the games
are policies, which are assumed to lie in some low-dimensional
Euclidean space; utilities to the players are usually defined in terms
of distance from their most preferred, or ideal, points. Such a
representation is most useful when the outcomes correspond to
public goods (so that several individuals might obtain similar benefits
from the same outcome) and so have been used mainly in
connection with applications to political science. We refer the reader
to Black [16.1], Plott [16.14], Owen [16.12], Ordeshook [16.11], and
Riker and Ordeshook [16.15], for some of the earlier developments.
16.1.1 Definition. An  -person voting game is a set
 of
players, called voters, together with a collection
 of subsets of
,
the winning coalitions. All other subsets of
 are called losing
coalitions. It is assumed that

(i)
(ii)
(iii) If
, and
, then

--- Sayfa 545 ---
The game will be called proper if, for any  and
.
It will be called decisive if, for any , either  or
.
We shall, in this section, deal exclusively with proper, decisive
voting games. Some of the results given here are valid for other
voting games, but it should not be assumed that this is so in general.
16.1.2 Definition. An  -dimensional spatial game is a voting game,
together with a collection of  points
, in
-dimensional
Euclidean space
. Point
 is voter i’s ideal point. The convex hull
of the points
 is the Pareto-optimal set.
Heuristically, the situation is simple enough. The voters are to
choose among alternative policies (or candidates) by voting; a
winning coalition can enforce any point on which its members agree.
It is assumed that player  would be happiest with a policy occupying
point
; failing that, he would like a position as close as possible to
, i.e., we assume that i’s utility is a decreasing function of the
distance between the achieved outcome and
.
The points of space

policies,  and , we define a partition of

.
The core is (as always) the set of undominated policies.
The following will be an important concept:
16.1.3 Definition. Let
 be an -dimensional spatial game.
The hyperplane

--- Sayfa 546 ---
is a median hyperplane for this game if the two sets
and
are both losing coalitions.
Essentially, a median hyperplane is a hyperplane such that the
ideal points to either side of it (i.e., in the open half-spaces) form a
losing coalition. For a decisive game, this can only happen if at least
one of the ideal points lies on the hyperplane. No two median
hyperplanes can be parallel, but there is one median hyperplane in
each direction in space; i.e., given any hyperplane in space, there
will be exactly one median hyperplane parallel to this. Note also that,
for a decisive game, if each open half-space contains only a losing
coalition of ideal points, then each closed half-space contains a
winning coalition.
It is not difficult to see that, when
, so that the voters can, in
fact, be ordered along a line, there will be a point in the core,
obtained as follows.
If the linear ordering is strict (no two ideal points coincide) there
will be a voter such that the set of all voters to either side of him
forms a losing coalition. For decisive proper games, he will be
unique — the median or swing voter. If the linear ordering is not
strict, so that several voters’ ideal points coincide, the swing voter
need not be unique, but there will be a unique swing position. This
position can easily be seen to lie in the core. (Note that, since the
space here has dimension 1, the median “hyperplane” is nothing
other than the median point.)
For
, and a fortiori for greater , the core is usually empty.
We see this in Figure 16.1, where points
 and  represent the

--- Sayfa 547 ---
ideal points for voters 1, 2, and 3 respectively. (In this three-voter
game, we assume each coalition of two or more voters wins.)
Figure 16.1.

In fact, it is possible for a two-dimensional array of voters to have
a non-empty core. Figure 16.2 illustrates this, for a five-voter game
(in which all coalitions of three or more voters win). The point here is

Figure 16.2.
(The above analysis suggests that core points exist only in
degenerate cases, i.e., two or more coincident points, three or more
collinear points, etc. In fact that is the case for symmetric games,
where each voter has only one vote. In general weighted voting
games, however, if one voter has many votes, a core point may exist
even under nondegeneracy. The obvious example occurs when one
voter (a dictator) has more than half the votes. In such case the
dictator’s position is clearly a core point.)
16.1.4 Theorem. A necessary and sufficient condition for a spatial
game to have a nonempty core is that all median hyperplanes pass
through one same point. That point (the intersection of all median
hyperplanes) is necessarily the ideal point of at least one voter.
Moreover, this point is not only undominated; it actually dominates all
other points.
Proof. Suppose that point  does not lie on a median hyperplane,

least one player. This is left as an exercise for the reader.
We see, then, that the core is frequently empty for higher-
dimensional games. This deprives us of one of the most appealing
solution concepts. We look, then, for points which come “as close as
possible” to being in the core. Three approaches seem reasonable.

(a) The Copeland Winner: The core consists of points which are
undominated by any other. Absent such a point, we might look for
a point
 which is dominated by “as few” points as possible. This
idea was originally advanced by Copeland [16.2]. In the context
of Euclidean space, we take this “as few” to mean a set with
minimal -dimensional Lebesgue measure (i.e., area or volume).
Figure 16.3 shows the same configuration as 16.1. Three circles
are drawn, passing through , and centered at the ideal points
,
and , respectively. Now,  is dominated by all points in the interior
of the three cigar-shaped areas which are bounded by these circles.
It dominates all other points, except for a set of area 0, namely,
points on the three circular arcs. It can be shown (see Problem 6)
that the total shaded area is then

For more than three voters, calculation of the Copeland winner

reader is referred to Grofman et al. [16.7] and to Owen and Shapley
[16.13] for a discussion of this problem.

(b) The Yolk: As discussed above, the core point, if it exists, must lie
at the intersection of all the median hyperplanes. When there is
no such point, we might look for the point which is closest to all
such planes, in the sense of minimizing its distance from them,
i.e., a point  which gives
where

,
and the maximum is taken over all median hyperplanes. Such an  is
the yolk center, and the ball of radius  centered at  (the smallest
ball which touches all median hyperplanes) is known as the yolk.
This concept was first introduced by McKelvey [16.8]; its principal
properties have been developed in Feld et al. [16.5].
In Figure 16.4, the same configuration of 16.1 is used once
again. The yolk here is the circle inscribed in triangle

--- Sayfa 551 ---
— the incenter of the triangle — can be found, as is well known from
elementary Euclidean geometry, at the intersection of the three angle
bisectors.
Figure 16.4.
For more complex configurations, locating the yolk is more
complicated. We note that there are an infinite number of median
hyperplanes, one in each direction in space. However, only a finite
number of hyperplanes need be considered in searching for . Since
distance from a hyperplane is a linear function of position, it follows
that the yolk can be found by linear programming. (The problem is
that it is by no means clear which hyper-planes need to be
considered.) Readers are referred to McKelvey [16.8], Ferejohn et al.
[16.6] for details.

(c) The Minimal Response Point: We mentioned above that the core
point (if it exists) will dominate all other points. In its absence, we
might look for the point which “comes closest” to dominating all
others.

16.2. The Modified Power Index
Alternatively, we might try to define a power index for spatial games.
With no spatial considerations, of course, there are well-known
power indices (the Shapley value or Banzhaf-Coleman index) which
can be applied directly to the set
 of winning coalitions for a given
game. (We merely define
 if
, and
 otherwise.) The
idea, however, is to modify such an index so as to take account of
the geometry of the points
.
As discussed in Section 12.l, above, the Shapley value can be
obtained by taking an arbitrary permutation (ordering) of the
players, and assigning to player
 the marginal contribution
 which  makes to the coalition
 of all players who
precede him or her in the ordering. The value is then the arithmetic
mean of these  marginal contributions.
In case of a voting game, this can be simplified by introducing the
idea of a pivot:
16.2.1 Definition. Let

linear ordering of

--- Sayfa 555 ---

coalition, while S (i; )

As a very simple example, if

more elements, then the pivot is always the
th element in the
ordering. More generally, the pivot is the first element (in the given
ordering) to complete a winning coalition. It is not difficult to see that,
by conditions (i–iii) of Definition 16.1.1 above, every ordering
(permutation) of the set  has a unique pivot. If the game is decisive,
then the pivot for ordering

ordering  (where a b if and only if b a).
If, now, we define

 otherwise,
we see that every permutation will have a unique player  (the pivot)
for whom the marginal contribution

this contribution is 0. Thus for a voting game the value

the number of permutations for which  is a pivot, divided by . This
can be interpreted as saying that

pivot, assuming a uniform probability distribution over the set of all
permutations of .
Suppose, however, that the points
 are introduced. It seems
reasonable that the geometry of these points should affect the
probabilities assigned to the various permutations. As an example,
consider a 3-person game (where
 consists of all coalitions of two
or more voters), where the ideal points are as in Figure 16.6.
Figure 16.6.

--- Sayfa 556 ---
In such a situation, since players 1 and 2 are very close together,
with 3 much further away, the two orderings 1–3–2 and 2–3–1
(corresponding to situations where 3 would agree with one of 1 and
2 against the other) would seem to be much more difficult to obtain,
and so we would be justified in assigning lower probabilities to these
two orderings than to the other four. But with the given voting rule
(any two or more players form a winning coalition), the pivot is
always the second player in any order. Thus
, the probability that 3
will be the pivot, would be considerably less than 1/3. Heuristically,
this is not unreasonable: since voters 1 and 2 have their ideal points
close together, they will tend to agree on most issues, and 3’s
opinion will usually have little effect on the outcome.
Now, how can we best develop this idea? A priori, there are many
possibilities. To choose among these, we might consider several
desiderata:
D1. If
 and
 are close together, then ceteris paribus,
permutations in which  and  are close together should have higher
probability than those in which these two players are far apart.
D2. The probabilities should be invariant under rigid motions of
the configuration of ideal points.
D3. The probabilities should be continuous functions of the ideal
points, except possibly for discontinuities when two or more of the
ideal points coincide, when three or more lie on the same line, etc.
D4. If two ideal points
 and
 coincide, the effect should be as
if the two players,  and , were consolidated into a single player.
Now, there may be many schemes which satisfy D1–D4, above.
The following is due to Shapley [1975]. First, Shapley defines issues
space as the dual,

functional over policy space

is that the gradient of the functional

--- Sayfa 557 ---

In general, it is interesting to see how this index measures voting
power. In a two-out-of-three majority voting game, the power
assigned to the three voters is proportional to the angles in the
triangle formed by their ideal points. Thus, in a situation such as that
of Figure 16.6, voter 3’s ideal point is very far from those of the other
two voters; the corresponding angle is consequently very small. It
follows that voter 3 has very little power there. On the other hand, in
a situation such as that of Figure 16.8, voter 2 is a “central” voter: his

--- Sayfa 560 ---
position is almost directly between that of the other two voters. The
corresponding angle is large (obtuse) and so voter 2’s power is quite
large. Heuristically, 2 seems to be in position to play off 1 and 3
against each other in such a case, forming coalitions with one or the
other with little danger that they will act together against him.
Figure 16.8.
In Example 16.2.6, we note that, given this particular voting
game, the power index depends only on the three angles of the
triangle; i.e., it will be the same for any similar triangle. This suggests
that, in general, this might be so for all voting games. In fact, the
following theorem tells us that this is indeed the case.

It is of interest to note that, for Example 16.2.10, the center of
power
 coincides with the Copeland winner
, as given by
equation (16.2). Note also that in 16.2.11, the center of power is in
fact the unique core point. As mentioned above, the core of a
decisive spatial game, if nonempty, consists of exactly one point
which is necessarily the Copeland winner. Thus for this example
also, the center of power and the Copeland winner coincide. The
question is whether this is merely a coincidence.
In fact, the following is true:
16.2.11 Theorem. Let
 be a proper decisive -dimensional
spatial game. Suppose
 or 2. Then the Copeland winner
 is
unique, and coincides with the center of power
.
For dimension
, the theorem is merely a restatement of the
fact, mentioned above, that the position of the median (pivot) voter is
the unique core point. For
, we omit the proof of this theorem,
which is quite lengthy. An outline of the proof is given in Problem 7 at
the end of this chapter. We note, however, that the Copeland winner
is the point minimizing a function which, however, is not itself easy to
determine. Thus a direct computation of the Copeland winner can be
quite complicated. On the other hand, computation of the power
index  is relatively straightforward in many cases. If nothing else, a
numerical approximation — calculating the pivot in a reasonably
large number of directions — can be done quite quickly with
computers. Since (at least for dimension 2) the two problems are
equivalent, we can simplify the Copeland winner calculation
considerably.

Chapter 17
Other Applications
17.1. Introduction
In this final chapter we shall consider certain applications of game
theory. We will not try to give a very rigorous, complete treatment of
these topics, but rather explain how game theory can be applied to
them.
17.2. Single-Item Auctions
Consider a situation in which an auctioneer wishes to sell an item.
There are n individuals called bidders. Each bidder i values the item
at a price θ i: this is known as her type. These types are independent
random variables, with uniform distribution over the interval [0, 1].
Each individual knows her own type, but nothing else. Bidder i sends
a message: a bid, bi. The auctioneer receives a vector b = (b1, …,
bn) of messages.
17.2.1. First-Price Sealed-Bid Auctions
In a first-price sealed-bid auction, each bidder sends her bid bi in
ignorance of the others’ bids. Once the bids are known, the high
bidder receives the item, and must pay the amount of his bid. The
payoff to bidder i is then

--- Sayfa 570 ---
Now, how should a given individual, say individual 1, bid? Clearly
she should not choose b1 >θ1 as she would thus risk a negative
payoff, and could never get a positive payoff. Now, if she makes a
bid b, the probability that she will be high bidder is equal to the
probability that all other n−1 bidders make lower bids. Of course 1
does not know how they will bid.
Assume, first, that bidders h, (other than 1) all bid their type: bh
=θh. Since the types are uniformly distributed in the unit interval, the
probability that a particular bh <b is simply b. Since the bids are
independent, the probability that bh<b for all h other than 1 will be
bn−1. Thus, the probability that 1’s bid of b will be high bid is bn−1.
Bidder 1 will then gain θ1 −b with probability bn−1 and 0
otherwise. Her expected winnings are therefore
It is straightforward now to maximize this expression: the
maximum is found at
There is one difficulty with this analysis, namely, it assumes that
all bidders other than 1 will in fact bid their types. In other words,
bidder 1 is the only one assumed to act strategically.
Assume, then, that all bidders, other than 1, make the strategic
choice bh=(n−1)θh /n. Let 1 make the bid b. Note that, if so, none of
them will bid more than (n−1)/n, and so it cannot be good for bidder
1 to bid more than this same amount.
Now, the condition bh<b is equivalent to θh <bn/(n−1). This has
probability equal to the smaller of bn/(n−1), and 1. Since this is true
for all h≠1, the probability that individual 1’s bid of b be the largest
bid is [bn/(n−1)]n−1 if b<(n−1)/n, and 1 otherwise. Thus the expected
payoff to 1, for b<(n−1)/n, becomes

--- Sayfa 571 ---
Since this is merely the positive constant [n/(n−1)]n−1 times the
previous objective function π(b), it follows that it must have its
maximum at the same point, i.e., bidder 1’s optimal reply is still
This analysis is clearly true for each one of the bidders. We find,
then, that the bids
constitute a Nash equilibrium for the bidding game.
This result depends on the probability distribution of the vector θ.
In a somewhat more general case, we will assume that the several θi
are identically distributed independent random variables. Let F(θ) be
the cumulative distribution of each one of these. We look for a
symmetric equilibrium. This should have the form of a function β:
individual h will bid bh =β(θh). It seems reasonable to assume that β
should be a monotone, strictly increasing function, so that an inverse
function β−1 will exist and also be monotone increasing.
Assume all bidders, other than 1, are using the equilibrium
strategy β. Let individual 1, with type θ, bid b. This will be the high
bid if for each the n−1 other bidders, β(θh)<b, or equivalently,
θh<β−1(b). Now the probability that these n−1 variables all be smaller
than a given θ will be the cumulative distribution function
and so the probability that 1’s bid of b be high is given by G(β−1(b))=
[F(β−1(b))]n−1. Thus, 1’s expected payoff will be

At the maximizing point, this derivative must be 0, and b=β(θ).

17.2.4. Second-Price Auctions
In a second-price auction, the bidding proceeds as before. Once
again, the high bidder wins the item. This time, however, the winner
must pay, not her own bid, but rather the second-highest bid.
Once again, we consider bidder 1’s strategy. We know her payoff
will be
where bh is the second-highest bid.
We claim, now, that bidder 1 should in fact bid her value: b1 =θ1 ,
as this strategy dominates all others.
In fact, consider a bid b>θ1. Let bh be the highest of the
remaining bids. Discounting the possibility of ties, there are three
possibilities: (1) bh>b>θ1; (2) b>bh>θ1; (3) b>θ1>bh. We show that, in
any one of these cases, the bidder would do at least as well (and in
some cases better) by bidding θ1 instead.
In case (1), bidder 1 is not the high bidder, so she would receive
a payoff 0 with either b or θ1.
In case (2), she would be high bidder with b, and receive the
payoff θ1−bh<0. With bid θ1 she is not the high bidder and so

--- Sayfa 574 ---
receives payoff 0.
In case (3), she is the high bidder with either bid, and so receives
the payoff θ1−bh with either b or θ1.
Thus, the bid θ1 is never worse than b, and sometimes better.
Suppose, on the other hand, that b<θ1. Once again discounting
the possibility of ties, there are three possibilities: (1) bh>θ1>b; (2)
θ1>bh>b; (3) θ1>b>bh.
In case (1), bidder 1 is not the high bidder, so she would receive
a payoff 0 with either b or θ1.
In case (2), she wins with bid θ1, and receives the payoff
θ1−bh>0. With bid b she is not the high bidder and so receives 0.
In case (3), she is the high bidder with either bid, and so receives
the payoff θ1−bh with either b or θ1.
We find, then, that with a second-price auction, the strategy of
bidding one’s value: bh =θh is a dominant strategy for all players.
Note that this does not depend on the probability distribution for the
value vector θ: the only thing required for our proof is that ties have
probability 0. This is a very strong result, as it means the bidders will
not have to carry out complicated calculations: they don’t even need
to know the distribution.
There remains the question of whether the principal — who
wishes to maximize the selling price for the object — should choose
a first-price or a second-price auction. We will consider the uniform
distribution over [0, 1].
Consider first the first-price auction. For the uniform distribution,
we find that the maximum of n variables has cumulative distribution
G(x)=xn. The density of this maximum is then g(x)=nxn−1 and it has
expected value E[max θ]=n/(n+1). Since the bid is b=(n−1)θ/n, the
expected selling price is [n/(n+1)][(n−1)/n]=(n−1)/(n+1).
Consider, next, the second-price auction. Let θh be the second-
highest type. In this case, we note that the event “θh≤x” can be
expressed as the union of the disjoint events: (1) “all θi≤x” and (2)
“one θk>x and all n−1 other θi≤x”. The probability of (1) is, as before,
xn. For (2), we note that there are here n possibilities, since the high

--- Sayfa 575 ---
bidder, k, can be any one of n. Thus, the probability of (2) is
n(1−x)xn−1. The cumulative distribution of θh is therefore
Some small calculations will then give us the expected value
E[θh]=(n−1)/(n+1). Since θh is the selling price, we find that the two
mechanisms give the same expected payoff to the principal.
We conclude that, if the seller’s utility for money is in fact equal to
the quantity of money (or, more generally, a linear function of
money), he should be indifferent between the two types of auction.
Of course there is no guarantee that seller’s utility is in fact a linear
function of money. With more complicated utility functions additional
calculations (which we will avoid here) would be necessary.
17.3. Mechanisms
Up to now, we have assumed that players play a game with well-
defined rules, and well-defined payoff functions (though in some
cases the players might not be certain as to these). In some cases,
however, the problem is to design a game so as to induce the
players to act in a certain way. We therefore consider a situation with
n+1 agents: a set N={1, 2, …, n} of players (known as individuals),
and an additional agent called the principal. This principal has an
objective function, and will be in a position to modify the individuals’
payoff functions so as to maximize (or minimize) this function. (Note
that, in the auctions described above, the principal is the seller or
auctioneer; in the case of Example 17.3.1 below, the principal is the
regulator.) We start with a very simple example, which gives us some
idea of how things may be done.
17.3.1 Example. Decoupling: Consider a service company,
delivering some type of utility to the public at large. The service can
be, e.g., water, gas, electricity. We will specifically consider an
electric service company. The players here the company and its

--- Sayfa 576 ---
customers, though there is an additional player, usually unseen: the
regulator.
In general these companies are regulated monopolies. A
regulator determines the rates that they can charge for their
services. These rates should be enough to cover both fixed and
variable costs, plus a reasonable rate of return to their investors.
Now, it is easy enough to calculate the variable costs of the service:
purchases of fuel, payments to suppliers and employees, and so
forth. What is more problematic is the calculation of payments for
fixed costs and investors’ return.
Usually, the regulator will calculate a, the variable costs per
kilowatt-hour, with little difficulty. He then calculates b, the fixed costs
authorized for depreciation on plant elements, interests paid to
bondholders, and return to stockholders, as well as c, the expected
sales (in kilowatt-hours) during a given time period. Thus he
authorizes a rate of
dollars per kilowatt-hour.
Now, if sales in the given time period are indeed equal to c
kilowatt-hours, the company will receive
dollars in the time period. Of this, ac will cover the variable costs,
while the remaining b will be equal to the income authorized to cover
fixed costs and investors’ returns.
Unfortunately, things do not always work out as planned. Actual
sales will depend not only on the rates but also on the aggregate
demand function of the remaining players (the firm’s customers), and
this is known only probabilistically. Moreover, the firm is generally
able (by advertising, say) to induce its customers to increase
consumption above the calculated amount c.
Suppose, then, that consumption is equal to d kilowatt-hours,
where d>c. In that case total receipts will be

--- Sayfa 577 ---
dollars. Thus, the firm receives ad to cover its variable costs, plus b,
its authorized returns for fixed costs, plus a last term, x(d–c), which
represents an unearned profit to the firm. Clearly it is in the firm’s
interest to increase demand.
It remains to determine the regulator’s objective function. This is
not easy to specify, but we should take into account that high
consumption will, on the one hand, cause environmental pollution,
and, on the other hand, require increased imports of fuel. It seems
reasonable, then, that other things being equal, the regulator prefers
consumption to be relatively low. Thus the regulator and the electric
company have conflicting interests: the regulator prefers low
consumption, while the firm will increase its profits with high
consumption. Since the firm can communicate with its customers —
something that the regulator is not allowed to do — it seems the firm
will succeed in increasing consumption.
There is, however, something that the regulator can in fact do: to
wit, he can modify the firm’s payoff function. This is usually done by
a technique known as decoupling.
In a decoupling mechanism, the firm’s rates are modified with
some regularity. Thus, to continue with the above example, suppose
that consumption, d, over a given time period was indeed greater
than the initial estimate, c. In this case, the firm would have received
an unearned profit k = x(d–c). If so, then for the subsequent period
the firm would be allowed, not the quantity b, but rather the quantity
b′=b−k, to cover fixed costs and returns to investors. The rate
allowed for the next time period would be
dollars per kilowatt hour, rather than a+b/c.
(Note that, if d<c, so that the firm receives less than b dollars in
the given period, then k<0, so that the regulator will allow the firm to
increase its rates for the next period.)
Thus any unearned profit in a given time period is taken back
from the firm in the subsequent period, and it is no longer in the

--- Sayfa 578 ---
firm’s interest to (induce its customers to) increase consumption.
There will no longer be a conflict between the regulator and the firm.
17.3.1. Description of Mechanisms
As mentioned above, we consider a situation with n+1 players, the
several individuals {1, 2,…, n}, and the principal. Each individual i
has a type θi which determines his utility function, his capabilities,
and such. (This θi may be a single number, a vector, or something
more complicated.) Let Θi be the set of possible types for that
individual. The individual is aware of his type (he knows what he
likes!) but does not know the other individuals’ types, knowing only
that they are determined according to a known probability
distribution. Let Θ be the cartesian product Θ1×Θ2×…×Θn. A type
profile is an n-tuple θ = (θ1,…, θn) ∈ Θ.
There are k types of assets, which are to be distributed among
the individuals. An assignment is a vector a = (a1, …, ak). An
allocation is an n-tuple x = (a1, …, an) of assignments. There is a
compact set A in n×k space, which consists of the feasible
allocations.
As an example, there may exist a vector C=(c1, c2, …, ck) of
available assets. In such case, A might consist of all non-negative
allocations (a1, …, an) such that ∑i ai = C. In some cases it may be
more complicated than that, possibly consisting only of integer-
valued assignments. There may be other types of restrictions.
A result is a pair [x, t], where x is a feasible allocation, and t=(t1,
…, tn) is a vector of monetary payments. The principal would like to
have the result be a function of the type profile θ — usually, a result
that satisfies some objective. However, the principal does not know
the types; rather, he will have to base his action on some messages
that the several individuals send.
A mechanism is a function y which assigns, to each n-tuple of
messages, a result.
At the beginning of the game, the principal promulgates the
mechanism y, i.e., the rule which will determine the allocations and

--- Sayfa 579 ---
payoffs.
Once the mechanism is known, each individual i announces a
message, mi, In many cases this is an element of Θi, though it may
be something different — possibly, something simpler. Frequently,
the individual claims that this message is indeed his type, θi. There is
however nothing to prevent the individual from lying.
Letting m = (m1, …, mn) be the profile (vector) of messages, the
mechanism y is then used to give a result
In other words, individual i will receive the assignment ai(m) and
must then pay the monetary amount ti(m). In many cases, of course,
ti < 0, so the individual actually receives some money.
The utility to a given individual is given by
Thus, the utility to an individual of a given allocation depends on his
type and assignment, but not on the other players’ types or
assignments. The value of the monetary payment is in fact equal to
the quantity of money (and independent of any assignment received.
This type of utility function is said to be quasi-linear in money (or
simply quasi-linear); money is frequently said to be the numéraire.
The advantage of this assumption lies in the fact that this allows for a
linear transfer of utility – monetary side payments are allowed. Thus
in general the principal would like to develop a mechanism that
maximizes the sum
over the set A. In other words, if all individuals can explain their utility
functions, and are truthful, the mechanism will maximize the total
[expected] utility. Any adjustments can then be made through the
side payments t. We will therefore define a social welfare function as

--- Sayfa 580 ---
a function f which assigns, to each type profile θ=(θ1, …, θn) an
allocation f (θ)=(a1 (θ), …, an (θ)) such that
Generally speaking, the maximization problem implied in the
social welfare function may be complicated, but reasonably
straightforward. In some cases it could be as easy as a linear
program. The problem, however, is that the vector θ = (θ1, …, θn) is
unknown: each individual i will deliver a message mi which he claims
is related in some way to his type θi but he may find it in his interest
to lie. Moreover the individual player may be unable to describe his
utility function properly: he knows whether he prefers one allocation
to another, but cannot give a total description of his type. The
problem faced by the principal consists in obtaining the outcome f(θ)
on the basis of the messages m = (m1, …, mn). We give one such
instance.
17.4. Public Goods
Consider a situation in which two individuals must pay for a public
good. For a level x of this good (i.e., if a sum of money x is invested),
the agent i (=1, 2) will derive utility

problem is that each individual knows only her own type, the
regulator knows neither type, and, as mentioned above, the
individuals may find it profitable to lie about this.
Suppose, however, that the regulator asks each agent to send a

It should be noticed, of course, that the two agents do not reveal
their types: they merely send a message which consists of a single
number. This of course presents a problem as, not knowing each
other’s type, the agents will find it difficult to find the equilibrium
strategies (messages). The hope is however that, if an equilibrium is

--- Sayfa 583 ---
not reached, they might have an incentive to adjust their messages
and in this way approach the equilibrium.
It is not always possible to design a mechanism that will (a)
induce the individuals involved to make truthful statements, and (b)
allow the principal to obtain a socially optimal result. Nevertheless
there are cases when this can be done. The following example
illustrates this. The reader is encouraged to read the literature for a
rigorous treatment and for other examples.

Thus, they do substantially better than at equilibrium. This is

do, in fact, represent these incentives. We have, then.
and we see that agent 1 will find it in his interest to decrease the
value of m1. Agent 2 would like to increase m2, but not by so much.
Thus, assuming each agent, i, modifies his message in proportion to
the incentive ∂Ui /∂mi, total investment will decrease.
In general, we can imagine that this type of situation will happen:
the two agents will, originally, send messages which are not efficient.

--- Sayfa 585 ---
Note, however, that if m1+m2>x*, then the sum of the two partial
derivatives, ∂U1/∂m1+∂U2/∂m2, will be negative, and so at least one,
and possibly both, of the agents will want to decrease her message.
Assuming the two agents modify their message in proportion to the
respective incentives (derivatives), the sum m1+m2 will decrease.
Conversely, if m1+m2<x*, the tendency will be for the agents to
modify their messages so that this sum increases. In either case we
can imagine the agents modifying their messages until the efficient
level, x*, is reached.
17.5. Two-sided Matching
In Example 10.4.5 we discuss the assignment game: in this game,
there are two disjoint sets of players, M and M’. The only truly
effective coalitions are one-player coalitions, and two-player
coalitions consisting of one player from each of these two sets. In
that example, the two sets consisted of sellers and buyers of houses,
respectively; each seller had only one house, and each buyer could
buy at most one house. We saw there that for such games the core
will always be nonempty, and can in fact be found in an efficient
manner by, e.g., the well-known Hungarian algorithm (Kuhn [17.10]).
Now, the assignment problem is, in effect, a linear programming
problem, and for such games, the solution to the dual problem can
be used to obtain at least one point in the core. But the linear
programming representation is due to the possibility of side
payments: the buyer gives a sum of money to the seller in exchange
for the house. But what if side payments were not allowed? Could
the core, then, be empty?
It is of course difficult to imagine — at least in our economic
system — how a market for houses could function without side
payments. There are however other types of two-sided matching
systems which might function without side payments. The most
obvious is a marriage market.
A marriage market consists of two sets, M={m1,…, mk} (the men)
and W={w1,…, wl} (the women).

--- Sayfa 586 ---
Each man, mi, has a preference relation, pi, on the set W′=W∪{0},
where “0” represents “remaining unmarried.” We write
to denote that man mi would rather marry wj than wh.
This pi is a strict linear order relation — it is transitive,
asymmetric, and complete:

(1) If wj pi wh and wh pi wr, then wj pi wr
(2) It is not possible that wj pi wh and wh pi wj
(3) For any mi and any two (distinct) wj and wh, eitherwj pi whorwh pi
wj

Additionally, for man mi, we set
These are the women he finds unacceptable: mi would prefer to be
single rather than marry any member of Di.
(Note that, by condition (3), we are explicitly ruling out the
possibility of ties. Our treatment could be modified to include these
possibilities, but we will not do so here.)
Similarly, each woman wj has a preference relation, qj, on
M′=M∪{0}. This is also transitive, asymmetric, and complete. She
also has a set Ej of unacceptable men:
A marriage assignment, now, is a one-to-one mapping μ from a
subset S⊂ Monto a subset T⊂ W. Now, μ(mi) = wj means that man
mi marries woman wj. If mi∉S, then this man remains single. In a
similar way, if woman wh ∉T, then wh remains single.
Since μ is one-to-one, two different men cannot marry the same
woman. Similarly, since μ is a function, the same man cannot marry

--- Sayfa 587 ---
two different women. On the other hand, several of the men may
remain single. Similarly, several of the women may remain single.
Note that, since μ is one-to-one, the inverse mapping μ−1 is
defined on T. We will extend μ from the set S to the set M by setting
μ(mi)=0 for mi∉S. Similarly we will extend μ−1 from T to W by setting
μ−1(wh) = 0 for wh∉T.
We will assume that each individual’s preference depends only
on his or her spouse. Thus, given two assignments λ and μ, mi
prefers λ to μ if and only if λ(mi) piμ(mi). He is indifferent between λ
and μ if and only if λ(mi)=μ(mi). (A similar statement holds for
women.)
We look now for the core of the set of all assignments. It turns
out that domination, as in Example (10.4.5) above, can only be
through one- or two-person coalitions. An assignment is dominated
via a single person if that person is married to someone he or she
dislikes. In other words, μ is dominated via the coalition {mi} if
μ(mi)∈Di, and it is dominated via {wj} if μ−1(wj)∈Ej.
We will say that an assignment is individually rational if it is not
dominated via any singleton coalition. We therefore consider only
individually rational assignments. For such assignments, domination
can only be through two-person coalitions consisting of one man and
one woman.
An assignment μ is unstable if there exists a two-person coalition
{mi, wj} such that

(1)
(2)

In other words, both this man and this woman would be happier if
married to each other than with their present spouses. The core is
the set of stable assignments.
The question then is whether there is at least one stable
assignment. In fact, the following algorithm, the deferred acceptance
method, which is due to Gale and Shapley [17.4], shows that the
core is always nonempty. There are two versions of this, one more
favorable to the men and the other to the women. We give the one

--- Sayfa 588 ---
that is more favorable to the men. We will assume that each of the
men finds at least one of the women acceptable, and each of the
women finds at least one of the men acceptable: none of the Di is
equal to W, and none of the Ej is equal to M. All individuals are
unmarried, prior to the start of the algorithm.
The algorithm consists of several stages.
Stage 1(a): Each of the men proposes marriage to his favorite
woman (the one that ranks first in his preference order).
Stage 1(b): Any woman who has received at least one proposal
from an acceptable man will accept the proposal of the man she
most prefers, among those who have proposed to her. She is then
engaged to that man. She rejects all other proposals.
Any remaining woman is said to be uncommitted. Any man
whose proposal has been rejected is uncommitted.
Stage 2(a): Any engaged man will remain engaged to that same
woman. Any uncommitted man will propose to his first choice among
those women who have not previously rejected him, assuming that
she is acceptable to him. He may not propose to any woman who
has previously rejected or jilted him. He will not propose to any
woman whom he finds unacceptable.
Stage 2(b): Any engaged woman who receives a proposal from
at least one man whom she prefers to her current fiancé will break
off her current engagement (jilt that man) and accept the proposal of
the man she most favors from among those who propose to her. She
rejects all other proposals.
Any uncommitted woman who has received at least one proposal
from an acceptable man will accept the proposal of the man she
most prefers, among those who have proposed to her. She is then
engaged to that man. She rejects all other proposals.
Any
man
whose
current
proposal
is
rejected
remains
uncommitted. Jilted men are now uncommitted.
Except as mentioned in 2(b), women will maintain their status
quo: any engaged woman remains engaged to the same man; any
uncommitted woman remains uncommitted.
Stages, 3, 4, etc. will be exactly as Stage 2. The process will
continue in this manner until each man either (a) is engaged, or (b)
has been rejected by all the women he finds acceptable.

--- Sayfa 589 ---
Since a man is not allowed to propose twice to the same woman,
and there are l women, then no man can propose more than l times.
Since there are only k men, there will be at most kl proposals. Thus,
the process will terminate after not more than kl stages. When the
process terminates, all the engaged couples proceed to get married.
All uncommitted men and women will remain single.
It remains to prove that the assignment thus obtained is stable.
First of all, the algorithm explicitly states that no man ever
proposes to any unacceptable woman, and no woman ever accepts
a proposal from an unacceptable man. Thus, the assignment
obtained is individually rational.
Suppose, then, that the assignment is unstable. There must then
be a pair (m i, wj) who prefer each other to their assigned spouses
(or unmarried status). This means, of course, that wj is acceptable to
mi. Since she is preferable to his wife, and men propose to women in
order of their preference, mi must have proposed to wj at some time.
This means that she either rejected his proposal when made, or
accepted his proposal but later jilted him. In either of these two
cases, it was because she had a proposal from some man, mh,
whom she preferred to mi. Now she is currently either married to this
mh, or married to some other man after jilting mh and possibly some
other men. However, every time she jilted a man, it was for a
preferable man. Since the preference relation is transitive, she
prefers her current husband to mi. Since the relation is asymmetric,
she does not prefer mi to her current husband.
We conclude that the current assignment is stable.
17.5.1 Example. Consider a marriage market with 4 men and 4
women. The men’s preferences are as follows:
and the women’s preferences;

--- Sayfa 590 ---
The algorithm will then proceed as follows:
Stage 1(a): m1 proposes to w1; m2 proposes to w2; m3 proposes to
w1; m4 proposes to w1
Stage 1(b): w1 accepts m4; w2 accepts m2
Current engagements: {m4, w1}, {m2, w2}
Stage 2(a): m1 proposes to w2; m3 proposes to w1
Stage 2(b): w2 accepts m3 and jilts m2
Current engagements: {m4, w1}, {m3, w2}
Stage 3(a): m1 proposes to w3; m2 proposes to w3
Stage 3(b): w3 accepts m1
Current engagements: {m4, w1}, {m3, w2}, {m1, w3}
Stage 4(a): m2 proposes to w1
Stage 4(b): w1 accepts m2 and jilts m4
Current engagements: {m2, w1}, {m3, w2}, {m1, w3}
Stage 5(a): m4 proposes to w3
Stage 5(b): no acceptances
Current engagements: {m2, w1}, {m3, w2}, {m1, w3}
Stage 6(a): m4 proposes to w4
Stage 4(b): w4 accepts m4
Current engagements: {m2, w1}, {m3, w2}, {m1, w3}, {m4, w4}.
At this point, all men are engaged. The engaged couples are now
married. We leave it to the reader to check that this is stable.
There is, of course, an alternative approach, in which, at each
stage, the women propose and it is up to the men to accept the
proposals they like. In that case, the algorithm would go as follows:

--- Sayfa 591 ---
Stage 1(a): w1 proposes to m1: w2 proposes to m3: w3 proposes to
m1: w4 proposes to m1
Stage 1(b): m1 accepts w1; m3 accepts w2
Current engagements: {m1, w1}, {m3, w2}
Stage 2(a): w3 proposes to m2: w4 proposes to m2:
Stage 2(b): m2 accepts w3
Current engagements: {m1, w1}, {m3, w2}, {m2, w3}
Stage 3(a): w4 proposes to m3
Stage 3(b): m3 rejects w4
Stage 4(a): w4 proposes to m4
Stage 4(b): m4 accepts w4
Current engagements: {m1, w1}, {m3, w2}, {m2, w3}, {m4, w4}.
The algorithm terminates here as all women are now engaged.
Once again the reader may check that this assignment is stable.
The marriage problem is one of the simplest types of matching
problems. Other types include:
1. Student-college matching
There are applicants, ai, and colleges cj. In this case, college cj
has a finite number rj of openings. Applicants will have preferences
over colleges, and colleges, over prospective students. This can be
treated much as the marriage problem. The easiest approach is to
modify the marriage problem as follows: each of the applicants is
treated as man; each of a college’s rj openings is treated as a
different woman. The algorithm described above is then carried out
as before.
Once again, the algorithm will give a stable matching, with one
proviso: the colleges’ preferences for an individual do not depend on
whether another individual has accepted. This might, however,
happen, as colleges will frequently seek diversity in their student
population, or might be looking for a football team, which would
mean that a particular quarterback is a good choice only if the

--- Sayfa 592 ---
college has not recruited another quarterback. (Other examples
might exist.) In such cases, there is no guarantee of a stable
matching.
2. The Roommate Problem
In this problem, there is only one set, M; each member i∈ M has
preferences over the remaining members of M. The problem is to
assign the individuals, two to a room.
In this case, there is no guarantee of a stable matching. In fact,
consider a case with 4 individuals. Suppose their preferences are
In this case, there is no stable matching. The reason is that
whatever player is matched with the “unlikeable” roommate, m4, will
be able to form a dominating coalition with one of the other two. If for
example the matching is { {m1, m2}, {m3, m4} }, then m2 and m3
would both be happier with each other than with their assigned
roommates. Thus the assignment is unstable. It is easy to check that
the two other possible assignments are also unstable.
